## 1. Background information on the procedure

## 1.1. Type II variation

Pursuant to Article of Commission Regulation (EC) No 1234/2008, AstraZeneca AB submitted to the European Medicines Agency on 29/07/2025 an application for a variation to the CHMP for Scientific Opinion.

The following variation was requested&gt;:

The following changes were proposed:

| Variation(s) requested   | Variation(s) requested                                                              | Type              |
|--------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------|
| C.I.6.a                  | C.I.6.a Addition of a new therapeutic indication or modification of an approved one | Variation type II |

Extension of indication to include treatment of adults and adolescents with hypereosinophilic syndrome (HES) for FASENRA, based on primary analysis from study D3254C00001 (NATRON); this is a multicentre, randomised, double-blind, parallel-group, placebo-controlled, 24-week phase III study with an open-label extension to evaluate the efficacy and safety of benralizumab in patients with HES; As a consequence, sections 4.1, 4.2, 4.4, 4.8, 5.1 and 5.2 of the SmPC are updated. The Package Leaflet is updated in accordance. Version 8 of the RMP has also been submitted. In addition, the Marketing authorisation holder (MAH) took the opportunity to introduce editorial and administrative updates to the PI and to update the list of local representatives in the Package Leaflet. Furthermore, section 6.5 of the SmPC was updated.

## Information on paediatric requirements

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No 1901/2006, the application included an EMA Decision(s) EMA/PE/0000253868 on the agreement of a paediatric investigation plan (PIP).

At the time of submission of the application, the PIP EMA/PE/0000253868 was not yet completed as some measures were deferred.

## Information relating to orphan market exclusivity

## Similarity

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No. 141/2000 and Article 3 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 847/2000, the MAH did not submit a critical report addressing the possible similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products because there is no authorised orphan medicinal product for a condition related to the proposed indication.

## Scientific advice

The MAH did not seek Scientific Advice at the CHMP.

## 2. Scientific discussion

## 2.1. Introduction

## 2.1.1. Problem statement

## Disease or condition

HES is a heterogeneous disorder, with varied organ system involvement and diverse symptoms that range in severity. The most common presenting signs and symptoms reflect cutaneous, pulmonary and gastrointestinal involvement, though any organ can be involved.

Human eosinophils are terminally differentiated leukocytes characterized by cytoplasmatic granules containing biologically active molecules, including eosinophil peroxidase, eosinophil cationic protein, major basic protein, and several cytokines, such as transforming growth factor (TGF)-ß. Eosinophils are produced in the bone marrow following the development of multipotent hematopoietic stem cells into eosinophil-committed progenitors (EoPs), which then differentiate into mature eosinophils. Once mature, eosinophils leave the bone marrow and enter the bloodstream, where absolute eosinophil count (AEC) normal value ranges between 0.05 and 0.5 × 10 9 /L (Caminati et al., 2024).

Eosinophilia is defined by increased eosinophils in the peripheral blood and is observed in several inflammatory and reactive conditions. Blood eosinophilia is defined by an AEC of more than 0.5 × 10 9 /L, whereas hypereosinophilia (HE) requires an AEC of ≥1.5 × 10 9 /L. In patients with persistent (or recurrent) HE, tissue infiltration by eosinophils and release of eosinophil-derived mediators and cytotoxic proteins may result in clinically relevant organ damage and thus a hypereosinophilic syndrome (HES).

The following table proposed by ICOG-EO in 2022 provides a clear distinction between Hypereosinophilia (HE) and Hypereosinophilic Syndrome (HES) (Valent et al., 2023).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

TABLE 1 Definition of Hypereosinophilia (HE) and of the Hypereosinophilic Syndrome (HES)

| Name/term                                     | Abbreviation   | Definition and criteria                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
|-----------------------------------------------|----------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Hypereosinophilia                             | HE             | ≥1.5 eosinophils ×10/L peripheral blood on two examinations (interval ≥2 weeks).ª Tissue HE may or may not be detected.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| Tissue hypereosinophilia                      | Tissue HE      | One or more of the following applies: a) the percentage of eosinophils in bone marrow section exceeds 20% of all nucleated cells, and/or b) a pathologist is of the opinion that tissue infiltration by eosinophils is extensive and/ or c) marked deposition of eosinophil granule proteins is found (in the absence or presence of tissue infiltration by eosinophils) |
| Hypereosinophilic syndrome                    | HES            | a) criteria for blood HE fulfilled and: b) organ damage and/or dysfunction attributable to tissue HEb and: c) exclusion of other disorders or condition as major reason for organ damage                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Tissue-restricted HES (organ- restricted HES) |                | a) tissue HE but criteria for blood HE not fulfilled and: b) organ damage and/or dysfunction attributable to tissue HEb and: c) exclusion of other disorders or conditions as major reason for organ damage                                                                                                                                                              |

Abbreviations: eMBP1, eosinophil major basic protein 1; EPX, eosinophil peroxidase; HE, hypereosinophilia; HES, hypereosinophilic syndrome(s). ²In patients with chronic myeloid leukemia (CML) or acute myeloid leukemia (AML), HE is defined by an absolute eosinophil count of ≥1.5× 10°/L peripheral blood and a relative eosinophil count of at least 10% (both for at least 2weeks).

bHE-related organ damage (damage attributable to HE): organ dysfunction with marked tissue eosinophil infiltrates or/and extensive deposition of eosinophil-derived proteins such as eMBP1 or EPX (in the presence or absence of marked tissue eosinophils) and typical clinical, histopathological and laboratory-based signs of HE-induced organ damage. When considering (establishing) the diagnosis HES is important to exclude all other etiologies as primary reason of organ damage.

When blood HE is not recorded in a patient with tissue HE and clear signs of HES, the (provisional) diagnosis of tissue-restricted (organ-restricted) HES can be established.

The MAH provided a definition for Hypereosinophilic Syndrome: HES is a rare disease defined by the association of hypereosinophilia (AEC &gt; 1.5 × 109 cells/L in the peripheral blood on 2 examinations at least 1 month apart and/or pathologic confirmation of tissue hypereosinophilia), with eosinophilmediated organ/tissue damage and/or dysfunction, provided other potential causes for the damage have been excluded (Roufosse et al 2022a).

Secondary hypereosinophilic syndrome is characterized by hypereosinophilia produced by reactive/non-clonal eosinophils secondary to an underlying medical condition and persisting for at least six months. The disorder can derive from non-neoplastic conditions (such as chronic infections and infestations, allergic reactions, intoxications, or autoimmune and chronic inflammatory disorders) or from neoplasms including non-myeloid malignancies, among others.

## Claimed therapeutic indication

Fasenra is indicated as an add-on treatment for patients aged 12 years and older with inadequately controlled hypereosinophilic syndrome without an identifiable non-haematologic secondary cause (see section 5.1).

Based on CHMP concerns during the assessment on higher drug exposure in patients below 35kg body weight the Marketing Authorisation holder agreed to amend the therapeutic indication as follows (please refer to the assessment report for details):

Fasenra is indicated as an add-on treatment for adult and adolescent patients aged 12 years and older weighing at least 35 kg with inadequately controlled hypereosinophilic syndrome without an identifiable non-haematologic secondary cause (see section 4.2 and 5.1).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Epidemiology

The prevalence of HES varies greatly, with estimates ranging between 0.36 and 6.3 cases per 100,000 people in the US (Crane et al 2010), and between 0.15 and 0.89 cases per 100,000 people in the UK (Requena et al 2021). The MAH stated that data on prevalence of HES in EU could not be found.

The major variants of HES are myeloproliferative, lymphocytic, and idiopathic. Lymphocytic and idiopathic variants account for the majority (90%) of cases of HES. Approximately 10% of HES patients have myeloproliferative disease, which has clinical features consistent with a myeloid disorder or have a specific gene mutation (eg, FIP1L1-PDGFRA fusion) (Roufosse et al 2022a, Curtis and Ogbogu 2016). Myeloproliferative HES occurs almost exclusively in males, whereas others (lymphocytic and idiopathic variant HES) appear to be equally distributed between sexes (Roufosse et al 2022a, Cheah et al 2014, Ogbogu et al 2009). HES primarily affects adults. In a US SEER analysis, median age of patients at diagnosis was 52.5 years, with incidence rising steadily into the 65 to 74 age group (Crane et al 2010). Paediatric cases are extremely uncommon; a large paediatric study found only 0.3% of children with eosinophilia met criteria for idiopathic HES (Cetinkaya et al 2023).

## Biologic features

Eosinophilic inflammation is an important component in the pathogenesis of HES. Eosinophils are a rich source of pro-inflammatory mediators (eg, eicosanoids, leukotrienes, cytokines) and granule proteins (eg, ECP, EPX, EDN, and MBP), which are released causing tissue inflammation and organ damage. Eosinophil differentiation from myeloid progenitors in the bone marrow depends on the action of 3 haematopoietic cytokines: GMCSF, IL-3, and IL-5. Among these cytokines, only IL-5 is specific for eosinophil differentiation, and it is known to be overproduced during eosinophilic proliferation (Roufosse et al 2022a). However, the underlying mechanisms of hypereosinophilia in HES patients have not been conclusively demonstrated.

HES is diagnosed when known causes of secondary hypereosinophilia are excluded. HES can be classified into several subtypes, including myeloproliferative, lymphocytic and idiopathic variants (MHES, L-HES and I-HES, respectively), based on distinct molecular characteristics: Myeloid HES (M-HES) is predominantly associated with the FIP1-like 1 (FIP1L1) platelet-derived growth factor receptor alpha (PDGFRA) fusion gene; in lymphocytic HES (L-HES), hypereosinophilia is driven by a clonal population of activated T cells that overproduce eosinophilopoietic cytokines, most notably interleukin (IL)-5; in idiopathic HES (I-HES) the mechanisms resulting in eosinophil expansion remain unknown.

## Clinical presentation and stage/prognosis

The varied clinical presentations of HES reflect their heterogeneous pathophysiology and lead to a difficulty in diagnosis. Organ damage may occur in HES irrespective of the underlying subtype. I-HES appears to be particularly associated with left-ventricular abnormalities, respiratory issues and gastrointestinal symptoms; M-HES with splenomegaly, anemia and fatigue; and L-HES with skin lesions/rashes and gastrointestinal symptoms.

Any organ system may be susceptible to the effects of sustained eosinophilia. Dermatologic involvement is the most common clinical manifestation (reported in 69% of patients; eg, urticaria, angioedema, pruritus), followed by pulmonary (44%; eg, asthma, sinusitis, rhinitis, cough, pleural effusion), gastrointestinal (38%; eg, vomiting, diarrhoea, abdominal pain), neurologic (20%; eg, vertigo, paraesthesia, aphasia), and cardiac (20%; eg, congestive heart failure, cardiomyopathy, myocarditis) manifestations (Shomali and Gotlib 2022, Ogbogu et al 2009). Most patients also have constitutional symptoms (eg, fatigue, fever, malaise, weight loss, night sweats), which can be debilitating. HES can have severe sequela depending on the organ involved. These include events such as eosinophilic myocarditis and fibrosis, cerebral thromboembolism, encephalopathy, peripheral neuropathy, arterial and venous thrombosis, blurred vision, dermatological lesions, pulmonary infiltrates/fibrosis, hepatic disease, and colitis (Roufosse et al 2022a).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The heterogeneity of the clinical manifestations and organ system involvement in children are similar to those observed in adults; in a retrospective analysis of adults and children with HES, the overall frequencies of dermatologic, neurologic, allergic, rheumatologic, and haematologic manifestations were comparable in both populations. Increased incidences of gastrointestinal symptoms as well as constitutional complaints, such as fever and fatigue, and haematologic and/or oncologic manifestations including thrombus, splenomegaly, lymphadenopathy, and neoplasm, however, were reported in children compared with adults (Williams et al 2016).

In a cohort of 49 French patients (18% of whom were myeloproliferative HES), the 5-year, 10-year, and 15-year survival rates were 100%, 87%, and 66%, respectively, with a predicted median survival of approximately 17.5 years. In general, patients who do not respond to steroids, who have cardiac or neurologic disease, or who have higher eosinophil counts tend to have a worse prognosis.

## Management

Guidelines for the management and treatment of HES are limited; those available, focus on the management and treatment of eosinophilia, depending on the underlying cause, and provide recommendations for evaluating eosinophil-associated end-organ damage. The goals of treating patients with HES are to prevent organ damage, reduce incidence of flares, relieve symptoms, and limit disease progression by controlling eosinophil-driven inflammation (Hwee et al., 2023).

Current management of patients who have active symptomatic HES is based on long-term systemic OCS (Ogbogu et al 2009), the exception being for the myeloproliferative variant of HES associated with the FIP1L1-PDGFRA fusion tyrosine kinase gene translocation, for which imatinib mesylate is approved and considered first-line therapy (Dispenza and Bochner 2018, Curtis and Ogbogu 2016, Khoury et al 2016). Although most patients (85% to 90%) with the non-myeloproliferative variant have an initial response to corticosteroids, their use is subsequently discontinued in approximately 40% of patients due to lack of efficacy and in an additional 20% of patients due to intolerance (Khoury et al 2018, Ogbogu et al 2009). Chronic use of corticosteroids leads to considerable morbidity, and attempts are made to reduce doses due to steroid toxicity and related AEs, (Buchman 2001). Steroid-sparing immunosuppressive agents such as hydroxyurea, IFN-α, and methotrexate (Dispenza and Bochner 2018) are often introduced as second-line agents, but these are also associated with poor tolerability.

Currently, mepolizumab 300 mg Q4W is the only biologic approved for adults with inadequately controlled HES without an identifiable non-haematologic secondary cause (Shomali and Gotlib 2022). Mepolizumab is an IL-5 antagonist, which binds to IL-5 thereby preventing it from binding to the IL5Rα complex on the eosinophil cell surface. In a Phase III registrational trial, mepolizumab reduced the proportion of patients experiencing 1 or more flares during the study compared with placebo, together with improvements in the time to first flare, annualised flare rate, and fatigue severity (Roufosse et al

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

2020). As not all patients experienced benefit with mepolizumab, there remains a significant unmet need in this patient population.

Williams et al 2016 reported that clinical manifestations were similar between children and adults with HES, with the exception of pulmonary involvement (excluding asthma), which was more frequent in adults, and gastrointestinal involvement being more frequent in children. Children receive treatament with corticosteroids, imatinib, hydroxyurea, IFNα, methotrexate, and anti-IL-5 antibodies similarly to adults Consequently, diagnostic and treatment algorithms developed for adults with HES are likely to be applicable to the paediatric population. However, many of these agents also have side effects that impact the paediatric patient's quality of life or can be difficult to tolerate (Ogbogu et al 2009); therefore, safe and effective therapies that target eosinophils are clearly needed (Bochner et al 2012).

## 2.1.2. About the product

Benralizumab is a humanised, afucosylated, IL-5Rα mAb that binds specifically to the human IL-5Rα on the target cell. In humans, the IL-5R is specifically expressed on the surface of eosinophils and basophils.

FASENRA (benralizumab) has been approved:

- as an add-on maintenance treatment in adult patients with severe eosinophilic asthma inadequately controlled despite high-dose inhaled corticosteroids plus long-acting β-agonists
- as an add-on treatment for adult patients with relapsing or refractory eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis.

## 2.1.3. The development programme/compliance with CHMP guidance/scientific advice

The HES clinical development programme consists of a single pivotal Phase III, randomised, DB multicentre, parallel-group, placebo-controlled study to evaluate the efficacy and safety of benralizumab compared to placebo in treatment of patients with HES (D3254C00001; NATRON).

Conclusions on the long-term safety of benralizumab in HES and the recommended Q4W dosing are supported by data from pivotal Phase III studies in patients with severe eosinophilic asthma, SIROCCO (D3250C00017), CALIMA (D3250C00018), and BORA (D3250C00021), and in a Phase III study in patients with EGPA (MANDARA [D3253C00001]).

The benralizumab Phase III clinical development programme was based on regulatory advice from US FDA and EU PDCO, as well as feedback from disease experts and patients.

The design and conduct of the NATRON study was based on the following guidelines:

- CPMP: 'Points to Consider on Application with 1. Meta-Analyses; 2. One Pivotal Study' (CPMP 2001).
- The FDA guidance 'Demonstrating Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness for Human Drug and Biological Products' was used for guidance on utilising one single pivotal study to support the substantial evidence requirement for effectiveness (FDA 2019).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.2. Non-clinical aspects

No new non-clinical data have been submitted in this application, which was considered acceptable by the CHMP.

In accordance with the Guideline on the Environmental Risk Assessment of Medicinal Products for Human Use (EMEA/CHMP/SWP/4447/00, Rev. 1, 2024), amino acids, peptides, and proteins are excluded from such requirements due to their low likelihood of presenting a significant environmental risk. Consequently, as Benralizumab is classified within the exempted category, the justification of the company for not providing an Environmental Risk Assessment is accepted.

Benralizumab is not expected to pose a risk to the environment.

## 2.3.2. Pharmacokinetics

## Population Pharmacokinetic Analyses

NATRON is a multicentre, randomised, DB, parallel-group, placebo-controlled, Phase III study in patients with HES. The study includes a 24-week DB period to compare the efficacy and safety of benralizumab with placebo. The study also includes an ongoing OLE period to assess long-term safety and tolerability of benralizumab. One-hundred thirty-four patients were randomised in 1:1 ratio to receive either benralizumab 30 mg or matching placebo Q4W (67 patients in the benralizumab group and 67 patients in placebo group).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

PK samples were collected from the 67 patients with HES from the benralizumab group (trough PK samples). The PK Analysis Set included only patients who received benralizumab during the 24-week DB period.

## Population Pharmacokinetic Modelling

Population PK modelling was applied for the characterization of the PK properties of benralizumab in patients with HES. A popPK model for benralizumab in HES patients at 30 mg SC Q4W is reported in \"Pharmacometrics Report (MS-2025-01)\". The scope of the model was to quantify the PK of the product in HES patients given the very sparse PK samples collected (only Ctrough), calculate drug exposure in HES patients and identify potential covariates.

A previously established 2-compartment model with first-order absorption and elimination in adult and adolescent patients with severe asthma (referred to as legacy model, Yan et al 2019) was utilized for informative priors for all PK parameters except for clearance (CL). The model was based on a previous study (Yan et al 2019) which reports a PopPK model of benralizumab in asthma patients, using informative priors from that study for the values of all parameters except (CL).

The final population PK model for benralizumab in patients with HES adequately described the observed data as shown in pcVPC plot (Figure 5), and is characterized by a two-compartment model with a first-order absorption from the SC dosing site and a first-order elimination pathway. In addition, the goodness of fit plots (Figure 7) show that the model compares well to the observed data.

## Absorption

In patients with HES, the estimated value of the absorption rate half life (kaTHALF) was 3.76 days (RSE%:5.52) with IIV (%CV) 83.9 (Table 8). It is noted that this value is heavily influenced by the informative prior specification of the asthma model.

## Distribution

In patients with HES, the estimated Vc and Vp of benralizumab were 3.12 L (RSE%: 2.89) and 2.52 L (RSE% 4.68), respectively. The IIV on Vc and Vp (%CV) were 24.9% and 45.2%, respectively (Table 8). Note that these values are heavily influenced by the informative prior specification of the asthma model.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Elimination

In patients with HES, the estimated CL of benralizumab was 0.220 L/day (RSE%: 3.85), and IIV on CL (%CV) was 24.8% (Table 3 and Table 8). Clearance was predicted to be approximately 24% lower in patients with HES compared to patients with severe asthma. Lower clearance affects drug exposure which should be about 32% higher in HES patients compared to asthma patients for the same dose. The more frequent dosing of the HES regimen (Q4W in HES versus Q4W for the first 3 doses followed by Q8W thereafter for asthma) should be also considered. the median individual predicted exposure parameters, at steady-state are reported as follows: 81563 μg/L·d for AUCss, 2953 μg/L for Cavg,ss, 3628 μg/L for Cmax,ss and 1967 μg/L for Cmin,ss.

## Effects of covariates on PK parameters

The asthma model included a few covariates, namely Body weight (WT) on CL, Vc and Vp and Antidrug antibody status (ADA) on CL. From these covariates only ADA was found to have clinically significant impact (exceeding 40%) on drug exposure at SS (AUC, Cmax and Cmin) in HES patients as shown in Figures 9, 10 and 11.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In an assessment of the impact of a number of covariates on the posthoc PK parameters of individual patients, potential differences in benralizumab systemic exposure between different sub-groups (e.g age, body weight, sex, ADA, race, ethnicity, Japanese and Chinese groups) were evaluated by comparing individual post hoc model predicted PK parameters (CL, Vss, t1/2 and AUCss) from final popPK model in patients with HES.

The median individual values for CL, Vss and t1/2 in patients with HES were 0.220 L/d, 5.28 L and 19.8 days, respectively. The median individual AUCss was 81662 μg/L·d.

Consistent with the established asthma popPK model, the median CL tends to increase with increasing body weight, increasing CL from 0.160 to 0.259 L/d at increasing weight from &lt;50 kg to ≥90 kg, respectively. Additionally, despite the small number of ADA-positive subjects (n = 8), those who tested positive for anti-drug antibodies exhibited a higher clearance (0.283 L/d) than ADA-negative individuals (0.211 L/d).

While most patients ranged from 18 to 65 years of age, with limited data in adolescents (4 patients were under the age of 18 years old) and seniors (over 75), no significant differences in median clearance were observed across different age groups. This suggests that the pharmacokinetics of benralizumab are unlikely to be affected by age.

For race and ethnicity, small differences were found in CL between the different categories.

Estimated typical CL is somewhat higher in males than in females, with median values of 0.235 L/d compared to 0.200 L/d, respectively. However, when sex was included as a covariate in the popPK model, this difference was not statistically significant.

Despite the limited data available for the Japanese population (n=5), the median CL values were similar across groups, with values of 0.200 L/d for Japanese subjects and 0.220 L/d for non-Japanese subjects. This finding suggests that benralizumab behaves similarly in the Japanese population compared to the global population.

Even with a small sample size for the Chinese population (n=4), the median CL values were similar across groups, measuring 0.247 and 0.218 L/d for Chinese and non-Chinese subjects, respectively. This indicates that benralizumab exhibits comparable pharmacokinetic behavior in the Chinese population relative to global populations.

No significant differences in CL were observed across the various hepatic markers, including TBL, ALP, ALT and AST as well as renal markers, specifically creatinine clearance.

Finally, concerning the disease marker of baseline eosinophil counts, most subjects fell within the normal range of 500 cells/μL or lower, with 57 participants having counts within this range. Despite this, no differences in CL were observed across the various baseline eosinophil categories, indicating that eosinophil levels do not impact the pharmacokinetics of benralizumab.

Similar to CL, Vss also tends to increase with increasing body weight, from 4.43 L for patients &lt; 50 kg to 6.95 L for patients ≥90 kg. Males and ADA-positive subjects appeared to have a somewhat higher volume compared to females and ADA-negative subjects, respectively. No pattern in Vss was observed for the remining covariates. Despite the limited data, no differences in Vss were observed between Japanese and Chinese patients with HES and the global HES patient population.

Results for terminal half-life are consistent across all sub-groups, suggesting that the half-life of benralizumab in patients with HES was not influenced by these covariates. The potential trend toward a higher half-life in non-Chinese patients should be interpreted with caution due to limited data for the Chinese subgroup.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Finally, AUCss results confirmed that the most influential covariate on benralizumab exposure was the total body weight, where AUCss decreases by 36% from 108036 for patients &lt;50 kg to 69682 μg/L·d for patients ≥90 kg. In addition, patients who were ADA-positive exhibited approximately 28% lower exposure compared to ADA-negative patients. Summary plots indicate a potential reduction in AUCss with increasing age; however, the limited data for the youngest and oldest age groups renders this relationship inconclusive.

It should be noted that all model PK parameters except CL suffered from very high shrinkage (56.9% to 97.6%) which could indicate that the posthoc estimates are not reliable and are biased towards the respective population values. The exception was CL which had shrinkage 13%.

## 2.3.3. Pharmacodynamics

Evaluation of benralizumab pharmacodynamic characteristics was based on the assessment of changes in blood eosinophil count over time and proportion of patients who reached certain thresholds of eosinophil count reduction over time.

In NATRON, pharmacodynamic analyses were performed on the FAS study population (see below) using data available from the 24-week DB period.

## Mechanism of action

Benralizumab is a humanised, afucosylated, IL-5Rα mAb that binds specifically to the human IL-5Rα on the target cell. In humans, the IL-5R is specifically expressed on the surface of eosinophils and basophils.

The antibody has been engineered to be afucosylated, which enhances its interaction with FcγRIIIa, expressed on immune effector cells on natural killer cells and macrophages. Benralizumab targets eosinophil precursors, eosinophils, and other cells expressing IL-5Rα in the circulation and residing in tissues for ADCC. Benralizumab induces rapid, direct, and near-complete depletion of eosinophils in the blood, airways, sputum, gastrointestinal tract, and bone marrow.

## Primary and secondary pharmacology

Depletion of eosinophils on peripheral blood is an important marker of biological activity of benralizumab and critical to the treatment of patients with idiopathic HES.

The PD effect of benralizumab on patients in NATRON was consistent with its effect observed in patients with severe eosinophilic asthma and EGPA, and in a Phase II study in patients with HES (Kuang et al 2019, 2025): this was an Investigator-initiated, single centre, Phase IIa, DB, placebocontrolled study. Twenty patients were randomised 1:1 to receive either placebo or benralizumab 30 mg SC Q4W for a 12-week DB period followed by an OLE period. The proportion of patients demonstrating ≥ 50% reduction in AEC at Week 12 (primary endpoint) was significantly greater in the benralizumab group compared with the placebo group (9/10 patients versus 3/10 patients, respectively, p = 0.02). At Week 12, patients treated with benralizumab also had significantly decreased bone marrow eosinophils and blood basophils. At Week 48, 9/14 patients receiving benralizumab were able to reduce HES background therapy (including OCS, IFNα, and hydroxyurea), 7 of whom were able to completely discontinue background therapy and were stable on benralizumab monotherapy. Eosinophil depletion was associated with clinical improvement and the ability to taper background therapy/therapies, including OCS, which was sustained in most patients for 48 weeks. Fourteen patients were followed in an OLE; 10 of those received benralizumab for at least 8 years. Peripheral eosinophil counts remained suppressed in all patients. Overall, long-term (&gt; 5 years) benralizumab therapy was effective and well tolerated in HES patients without significant adverse effects. The MAH claims that the phase IIa study is characterized as a positive proof-of-concept for benralizumab in patients with PDGFRA-negative HES.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In NATRON, blood eosinophil depletion was seen at the first observed timepoint (Week 4) and was maintained over the 24-week DB period. Although the number of adolescent patients in the study was small, similar trends in sustained eosinophil depletion with benralizumab treatment were observed in this age group.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The following PD conclusions were made from the NATRON study:

- Between screening and randomisation, patients underwent a per protocol corticosteroid responsiveness assessment, which impacted blood eosinophil levels in both benralizumab and placebo groups (screening median AEC: 1085 cells/μL and 1120 cells/μL, respectively; randomisation/baseline median AEC: 140 cells/μL and 100 cells/μL, respectively) (Table 14.1.9.1 &amp; Figure 1).
- Following initiation of randomised therapy, median eosinophils continued to decrease in the benralizumab group compared to an increase in the placebo group at the first post-baseline timepoint (Week 4 median AEC: 30 cells/μL versus 580 cells/μL, respectively). The near complete depletion of median blood eosinophils in the benralizumab group was maintained over 24 weeks while eosinophil levels remained elevated in the placebo group (Week 24 median AEC: 25 cells/μL versus 510 cells/μL, respectively) (Figure 1 &amp; Table 14.2.13.1).

## Immunogenicity Results

The ADA prevalence (ie, the percentage of patients with positive ADA result at any time) was 11.9% and the ADA incidence (ie, the percentage of patients who were treatment-emergent ADA positive) was 10.6% in benralizumab-treated patients (Table 4). The ADA prevalence and incidence in placebotreated patients was 13.6% and 12.3%, respectively.

The ADA responses during the NATRON study are summarised in Table 4.

- [f] Treatment induced ADA positive is defined as ADA negative at baseline and at least one post-baseline ADA positive assessment.
- [g] Treatment-boosted ADA positive is defined as ADA positive at baseline and the baseline titre is boosted by greater than the variability of the assay (i.e. &gt; = 4-fold) at &gt; = 1 post-baseline assessment.
- [h] Non-treatment emergent ADA positive is defined as ADA positive but not fulling the conditions above for treatment-emergent ADA positive.
- [i] Persistently positive ADA is defined as ADA negative at baseline and having &gt; = 2 post-baseline ADA positive assessments (with &gt; = 16 weeks between first and last positive) or positive at the last available post baseline assessment.
- [i] Transiently positive ADA is defined as ADA negative at baseline and &gt; = 1 post-baseline ADA positive assessment but not fulfilling the conditions of persistently positive.
- [k] The median of maximum titre is calculated based on the maximum titre of each ADA positive patient (including both baseline and post-baseline measurements).
- [1] nAb prevalence is defined as nAb positive at any visit including baseline and/or post-baseline.
- [m] nAb incidence is defined as nAb negative at baseline (or ADA negative at baseline) and nAb positive at any post-baseline visit.

On-study DB period is defined as starting on or after day of first DB IP dose up to the day prior first OLE IP inclusive for patients who roll over to the OLE, or up the scheduled IPD visit for patients who do not roll over to the OLE, or last contact date for patients who exit the study, or data cut-off date.

The percentage of ADA-positive patients increased from 4.5% at baseline to 10.4% at Week 16 and remained stable through Week 24. Seroconversion occurred at Week 16.

During the 24-week DB period, for ADA-positive patients in the benralizumab group, the median ADA titre peaked at Week 4. However, there was an insufficient number of ADA-positive patients to draw any conclusions on the magnitude of ADA response.

The incidence of nAb in the benralizumab group was 3.0% (Table 4). In the benralizumab group, the percentage of patients who were nAb-positive at baseline was 1.5% (1/66) and remained relatively stable through the 24-week DB period. Three patients in the benralizumab group were nAb positive during the DB period, with 1 patient being persistently nAb positive and 2 patients being transiently nAb positive.

## Impact of Immunogenicity on PK

In the NATRON study, the number of ADA-positive patients was insufficient to draw meaningful conclusions regarding the impact of ADA on PK. However, there was a trend for lower geometric mean trough serum concentrations in ADA-positive patients with high titre and nAb positivity. In addition, greater variability in geometric mean trough serum concentrations was observed in ADA-positive patients

The impact of ADA on the PK of benralizumab was assessed in the popPK analysis based on pooled PK data from NATRON. Based on the results, CL of benralizumab was higher when patients were ADA positive (allometric coefficient: 2.34).

## Impact of Immunogenicity on PD

In the NATRON study, the number of ADA-positive patients was insufficient to allow a formal assessment of the impact of ADA on PD. However, in nAb-positive patients in the benralizumab group, a trend toward a less robust eosinophil depletion was observed, which was transient in nature.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Impact of Immunogenicity on Efficacy

In the NATRON study, no effect of ADA on efficacy was observed; however, the number of ADA positive patients was small and therefore a firm conclusion could not be drawn.

## Impact of Immunogenicity on Safety

In the NATRON study, there was no meaningful effect of ADA positive status on the overall incidence of AEs, and there was no apparent correlation between hypersensitivity AEs and ADA; however, the number of ADA positive patients was small and therefore a firm conclusion could not be drawn.

There were no clinically meaningful differences observed in AE or SAE rates between patients who were ADA-positive (treatment-emergent and non-treatment-emergent) compared with those who were ADA-negative. Five (71.4%) patients in the benralizumab group who were treatment-emergent ADApositive reported AEs during the 24-week DB period, compared with 38 (64.4%).

In conclusion, although the number of ADA-positive patients from the NATRON study was insufficient to allow formal assessments of the impact of ADA, there was no evidence of a consistent effect of ADA on PK or PD. Additionally, no evidence of an effect of ADA on efficacy or safety was observed.

## 2.3.4. PK/PD modelling

Not applicable

## 2.3.5. Discussion on clinical pharmacology

## Bioanalytical methods

Immunogenicity of benralizumab was evaluated using validated assays.

## popPK model

The popPK model for benralizumab in HES used a two-compartment structure with first-order absorption and elimination and relied on informative priors from an asthma model for all parameters except clearance to compensate for very sparse HES PK data (only trough samples). The modelling approach is considered reasonable and acceptable, however the heavy reliance on priors indicates that many parameter estimates are strongly influenced by the asthma model rather than by HES data. Clearance (CL) was estimated to be ~24% lower in HES versus asthma, leading to an expected ~32% higher drug exposure (AUCss) in HES for the same dose. The report suggests this result could potentially relate to the more frequent dosing regimen in HES (30 mg SC Q4W), yet that causal link needs further clarification, and the MAH was asked to justify or quantify this statement, as well as discuss the clinical relevance of the potentially elevated exposure. The MAH argued that the apparent ~24% reduction in CL is likely a methodological artifact arising from Ctrough only sampling rather than a true physiological difference, with the value remaining well within the 24.2% inter-individual variability seen in asthma. Furthermore, clinical data confirms that this potential ~32% increase in exposure does not impact efficacy or safety, as exposure-response analyses show no significant relationship with HES flares, and the 30 mg Q4W regimen mirrors the established safety profile of the Q8W regimen. The MAH's clarification was considered acceptable.

Post hoc assessments showed only minor variations in PK across body weight, age, sex, ADA status, race/ethnicity, hepatic and renal markers, and baseline eosinophils, with body weight and ADA having the largest effects on exposure. However, nearly all model parameters except CL exhibited very high shrinkage (57%-98%), suggesting that post hoc individual estimates are likely biased toward population means and potentially not reliable; the impact of this shrinkage on covariate conclusions and subgroup inferences should be acknowledged and discussed. The MAH addressed the uncertainties regarding the rationale for attributing lower CL to dosing frequency, the clinical implications of ~32% higher exposure in HES and discussed the high shrinkage and potential limitations in posthoc estimates suggesting that post hoc individual estimates are likely biased toward population means and potentially not reliable. The MAH was asked to acknowledge and discuss the impact of shrinkage on covariate conclusions and subgroup inferences.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The MAH acknowledged that while high shrinkage limits the reliability of subgroup inferences for volume of distribution (Vss) and half-life (t1/2), shrinkage for CL remained low because steady-state trough samples provide sufficient information to identify this parameter. Consequently, the population conclusions regarding CL, AUCss, and the minimal impact of demographic covariates on exposure are considered robust and mathematically justified. The MAH's discussion regarding parameter shrinkage was considered acceptable.

## Pharmacodynamic findings

Benralizumab's PD effects in NATRON align with its known mechanism: an afucosylated IL-5Rα monoclonal antibody engineered to enhance ADCC and produce rapid, near-complete depletion of eosinophils in blood and tissues. In NATRON this translated to a rapid decrease in peripheral blood eosinophils by Week 4 that was sustained through Week 24 in the benralizumab arm versus persistent elevation in placebo. The Phase IIa HES experience reported by the MAH (Kuang et al.2019, 2025) also showed eosinophil suppression and clinical improvements in patients treated with the same dosing regimen as patients in NATRON.

Several limitations affect interpretation. Screening AEC values decreased substantially by randomization because of a per-protocol corticosteroid responsiveness assessment, and therefore baseline AEC deviate from typical HES diagnostic thresholds, which complicates generalizability. Whilst this does not impede the overall conclusions on this application, the Phase IIa study that the MAH cites is small and heterogeneous (some patients overlap with EGID/EGPA), limiting firm conclusions about long-term PD effects or clinical translation.

ADA prevalence and incidence in the trial were low; however, ADA and nAb detection rates were comparable and even higher in the placebo group than in benralizumab groups, raising concerns about assay specificity; the MAH was asked to provide additional data to demonstrate whether the positive placebo values are assay artifacts or pre-existing antibodies and whether any confirmed ADA/nAb results have PD or safety relevance.

The MAH confirmed that the ADA method was selective and specific for the detection of ADA to benralizumab in the HES population, with no marked matrix effects. The MAH argued that the higher incidence of ADAs and nAbs within the placebo arm could possibly be related to patients' previous treatment history with other biologic therapy. The AEs reported in ADA/nAb positive patients do not raise safety concerns. ADA response in ADA positive subjects from the placebo group was lower compared with the responses from ADA positive subjects in the benralizumab group.

The comparative key immunogenicity data provided by the MAH for NATRON vs Phase III Asthma Exacerbation Studies and NATRON vs MANDARA (EGPA indication, not placebo-controlled study) demonstrated similarities in the benralizumab arms across studies. Differences in ADA incidence and nAb prevalence rates in the placebo group between the NATRON study and the combined Phase III asthma studies were attributed to the determination of immunogenicity assay cutpoints. In the severe asthma studies immunogenicity study-specific screening and confirmatory cutpoints were derived from patients enrolled in the studies, whereas in NATRON the respective cutpoints were derived from a commercial source.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.3.6. Conclusions on clinical pharmacology

Clinical pharmacology aspects of benralizumab effect on the new target population are in general adequately characterised. The popPK model is acceptable but appeared heavily driven by priors from asthma data. The apparent ~24% reduction in CL is however likely a methodological artifact arising from Ctrough only sampling rather than a true physiological difference, with the value remaining well within the 24.2% inter-individual variability seen in asthma. Furthermore, clinical data confirm that this potential ~32% increase in exposure does not impact efficacy or safety, as exposure-response analyses show no significant relationship with HES flares, and the 30 mg Q4W regimen mirrors the established safety profile of the Q8W regimen.

Pharmacodynamic data demonstrated rapid and sustained eosinophil depletion consistent with benralizumab's mechanism of action.

The MAH confirmed the specificity of the method for ADA determination in the HES population. The higher immunogenicity in placebo group in the NATRON is potentially linked to prior biologic therapies and the methodology for determination of assay cutpoints. Furthermore, comparable immunogenicity results were demonstrated between treatment arms across phase III asthma exacerbation and EGPA studies with benralizumab, with no safety concerns.

## 2.4. Clinical efficacy

## 2.4.1. Dose response study(ies)

Given the rarity of the condition, a formal dose-ranging study in HES was not considered feasible. The rationale for a single-study clinical development program evaluating a single benralizumab dosing regimen of 30 mg SC Q4W was selected in HES on the basis of the fully characterized PK profile, PD effects of eosinophil depletion in blood and tissue, a Phase II Investigator-initiated study, and the extensive safety database.

The PK and PD of benralizumab are well-characterized (Wang et al 2017), demonstrating consistent PK across different disease populations. The approved dosing regimen of benralizumab in severe asthma is 30 mg administered SC Q4W for the first 3 doses, and Q8W thereafter. HES, however, has a higher eosinophil burden than asthma. As depletion of eosinophils in the tissues and circulation is important for control of HES, the higher frequency of dosing of 30 mg Q4W was selected to realize higher benralizumab serum levels and achieve near complete eosinophil depletion.

It is stated that the effectiveness and safety of the 30 mg Q4W dosing regimen was investigated in an Investigator-initiated Phase II study of patients with HES (Kuang et al 2019). This study was DB placebo-controlled for 12 weeks, followed by an OLE period. The higher benralizumab concentration in serum at steady state was effective in reducing blood and tissue eosinophilia in patients with varied clinical subtypes of HES, with an acceptable safety profile.

The PK/PD relationship of adolescents with asthma has been shown to be consistent with those of adults (SIROCCO [Bleeker et al 2016] and CALIMA [FitzGerald et al 2016]) and given that PK/PD

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

relationships are consistent across disease populations, the same benralizumab 30 mg SC Q4W was administered to adolescents with HES.

## 2.4.2. Main study

## NATRON study

A Multicentre, Randomised, Double-blind, Parallel-group, Placebo-controlled, 24 Week Phase III Study With an Open-label Extension to Evaluate the Efficacy and Safety of Benralizumab in Patients With Hypereosinophilic Syndrome (HES).

## Methods

Study D3254C00001[NATRON], hereafter referred to as NATRON is a 24-week, DB, placebo-controlled period followed by an OLE period of at least 1 year for adults and 2 years for adolescents aged ≥ 12 years (Figure 1). The DB portion of this study was designed to compare the efficacy and safety of benralizumab to placebo while the OLE period will assess long-term efficacy, safety, and tolerability of benralizumab in this patient population with chronic disease.

Figure 1 Study Design

<!-- image -->

label treatment

- a All patients remained on stable background HES medications during screening and throughout the DB period; background medications could be modified if a patient had a HES worsening/flare or an AE thought to be due to background therapy.
- b Three months after entering the OLE (Week 36), background HES medication could be adjusted.
- c Screening AEC were assessed at Visit 1 in local laboratory; results had to be available the same day. Potentially eligible patients (AEC ≥ 1000 μL) received 2 days of OCS to assess corticosteroid responsiveness (additional test for AECs at Visit 2, defined as an AEC &lt; 1000 cells/μL).
- d Visit 2 and Visit 3 (randomisation visit) could be performed the same day, if feasible, given that local laboratory results for corticosteroid responsiveness were obtained and all eligibility criteria confirmed prior to randomisation.
- e Primary database lock: The DCO for the primary database lock was planned to occur when approximately 38 patients had their first HES worsening/flare event during the DB period and all randomised patients had the opportunity to be followed up for the 24-week DB treatment period.
- f The final database lock will occur after the last patient completes the OLE

This study comprised 2 distinct periods: A 24-week DB treatment period, during which patients were to be randomised to receive either benralizumab or placebo in addition to prior stable HES background therapy, and an OLE treatment period, during which all patients were to receive exclusively benralizumab. The primary database lock (DBL) was planned to occur when approximately 38 patients had their first HES worsening/flare event during the DB treatment period and all randomised patients had the opportunity to be followed up for the 24-week DB treatment period.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Visit 9 (week 24) was planned to be the last visit of the DB period and the first visit of the OLE period, where patients would receive IP (benralizumab). All assessments at Visit 9 were planned to be performed before administration of open-label benralizumab. Following completion of the DB treatment period, adult patients had the opportunity to participate in the OLE treatment period for at least 1 year and adolescents for at least 2 years. The duration of each patient's participation after the end of Year 1 of the OLE was variable. The final DBL of NATRON was planned to occur after the last patient completed OLE period and as of submission date the OLE period of the study was ongoing.

## Study participants

## Inclusion criteria

## Informed Consent/Age/Gender

1. Provision of the signed and dated written informed consent of the patient or the patient's legally authorised representative, and informed assent from the patient (per local regulations) prior to any mandatory study-specific procedures, sampling, and analyses.
2. Males and females 12 years of age and older at the time of signing the ICF.

## Type of Patients and Disease Characteristics

3. Documented diagnosis of HES (history of persistent eosinophilia &gt; 1500 cells/μL without secondary cause on 2 examinations [interval ≥ 1 month; Valent et al 2012] and evidence of end organ manifestations attributable to the eosinophilia).
4. Documented negative testing for the FIP1L1-PDGFRA fusion tyrosine kinase gene translocation.
5. Stable HES treatment dose(s) and regimen for ≥ 4 weeks at the time of Visit 1.
6. Signs or symptoms of HES worsening/flare and/or laboratory abnormalities indicative of HES worsening/flare (other than isolated eosinophilia) at Visit 1

## OR

A documented history of 2 or more HES worsening/flares within 12 months prior to Visit 1 requiring an escalation in therapy.

- At least one flare within the past 12 months must not be related to a decrease in HES therapy during the 4 weeks prior to the flare.
7. AEC ≥ 1000 cells/μL at Visit 1 (assessed by local laboratory).
8. Corticosteroid responsiveness defined as an AEC &lt; 1000 cells/μL after a 2-day course of OCS (prednisone/prednisolone) 1 mg/kg/day at Visit 2 (assessed by local laboratory). Other OCSs in equivalent doses were permitted.

## Corticosteroid Responsiveness

An initial measurement of blood eosinophil levels was planned to be performed at Visit 1 and analysed by the local laboratory to determine if the AEC was ≥ 1000 cells/μL as per inclusion criterion 7. An AEC test taken within 3 days of Visit 1 could be accepted provided that it had been performed at the same local laboratory that wοuld be used for further testing during the screening period.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

At the end of Visit 1, the patients who were deemed to be potentially eligible, pending confirmation of inclusion criteria 7 and 8, would be provided a 2-day supply of OCS (prednisone/prednisolone) at a dose of 1 mg/kg/day. The site staff would contact patients as soon as the result for the screening AEC was obtained from the local laboratory (same day):

- If the local laboratory Visit 1 AEC result was ≥ 1000 cells/μL, patients were instructed to take the dispensed OCS for 2 days starting the next morning (screening Day 2) with the second dose taken the morning of screening Day 3 (Visit 2). This dose would have to be taken on top of the patient's regular background medications (including OCS).
- If the AEC was &lt; 1000 cells/μL, the patient would be considered screen failed (as per inclusion criterion 7) and was instructed to return or discard the dispensed OCS.

## Screening Day 3 (Visit 2)

Patients would return to the site on the second day of taking dispensed OCS. A local laboratory eosinophil test to assess corticosteroid responsiveness should be taken 4 to 8 hours after the second OCS (prednisone/prednisolone) dose.

- If the AEC was &lt; 1000 cells/μL, at screening Day 3 (Visit 2), the patient will be deemed corticosteroid responsive and meeting inclusion criterion 8.
- If the AEC remains ≥ 1000 cells/μL, the patient would be considered screen failed.

## Reproduction

9. WOCBP must agree to use a highly effective method of birth control (confirmed by the investigator) from enrolment, throughout the study duration, and within 12 weeks after last dose of IP and have a negative urine dipstick pregnancy test result on Visit 1.

Highly effective methods of birth control (those that can achieve a failure rate of less than 1% per year when used consistently and correctly) include:

- (a) Combined (oestrogen- and progestogen-containing) hormonal contraception associated with inhibition of ovulation: oral, intravaginal, or transdermal,
- (b) Progestogen-only hormonal contraception associated with inhibition of ovulation: oral, injectable, or implantable,
- (c) Intrauterine device,
- (d) Intrauterine hormone-releasing system,
- (e) Bilateral tubal occlusion,
- (f) Sexual abstinence, ie, refraining from heterosexual intercourse (the reliability of sexual abstinence needs to be evaluated in relation to the duration of the clinical study and the preferred and usual lifestyle of the patient),
- (g) Vasectomised sexual partner (provided that partner is the sole sexual partner of the WOCBP study patient and that the vasectomised partner has received medical assessment of the surgical success).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

10. Women not of childbearing potential are defined as women who are either permanently sterilised (hysterectomy, bilateral oophorectomy, or bilateral salpingectomy) or who are postmenopausal. Women will be considered postmenopausal if they have been amenorrhoeic for ≥ 12 months prior to the planned date of enrolment without an alternative medical cause. The following age-specific requirements apply:
2. (a) Women &lt; 50 years old will be considered postmenopausal if they have been amenorrhoeic for 12 months or more following cessation of exogenous hormonal treatment and FSH levels are in the postmenopausal range. Until FSH is documented to be within menopausal range, the patient should be treated as a WOCBP.
3. (b) Women ≥ 50 years old will be considered postmenopausal if they have been amenorrhoeic for 12 months or more following cessation of all exogenous hormonal treatment.

## Other

11. French patients: In France, a patient will be eligible for inclusion in this study only if either affiliated to, or a beneficiary of, a social security category.

## Exclusion Criteria

## Medical Conditions

1. Life-threatening HES and/or HES complication(s) as judged by the investigator:
2. (a) Medical intervention for HES-related life-threatening event(s) within 12 weeks prior to randomisation.
3. (b) History of thrombotic complications, stroke, or significant cardiac damage related to HES, if the respective events were life threatening and currently represent a risk of life-threatening disease complications. Events that occurred in the past but considered resolved or stable, can be accepted if, as per investigator's judgment, participation in the study will not put the patient at risk.
4. (c) Disease severity that in the opinion of the investigator makes the patient inappropriate for inclusion in the study.
2. Presence of FIP1L1-PDGFRA fusion tyrosine kinase gene translocation or other known imatinibsensitive mutation.
3. Definitive diagnosis of eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis.
4. Known, preexisting, clinically significant endocrine, autoimmune, metabolic, neurological, renal, gastrointestinal, hepatic, haematological, respiratory, or any other system abnormalities not associated with HES, uncontrolled with standard treatment which, in the opinion of the investigator, may put the patient at risk because of his/her participation in the study, or may influence the results of the study, or the patient's ability to complete the entire duration of the study.
5. Hypereosinophilia of unknown significance.
6. Cardiovascular: Documented history of any clinically significant cardiac damage, clinically significant echocardiography (if available) or ECG findings within 12 months prior to Visit 1 or clinically significant ECG findings at screening that in the opinion of the investigator may put the patients at risk.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

7. Known currently active liver disease.
2. (a) Chronic stable hepatitis B and C (including positive testing for hepatitis B surface antigen or hepatitis C antibody) or other stable chronic liver disease are acceptable if patient otherwise meets eligibility criteria. Stable chronic liver disease should generally be defined by the absence of ascites, encephalopathy, coagulopathy, hypoalbuminemia, oesophageal or gastric varices, or persistent jaundice, or cirrhosis.
3. (b) ALT or AST level ≥ 3 × ULN during the screening period (AST or ALT &gt; 5 × ULN if documented HES with liver manifestations). Transient increase of AST/ALT level that resolves by the time of randomisation is acceptable if, in the investigator's opinion, the patient does not have an active liver disease and meets other eligibility criteria.
8. Current or history of malignancy within 5 years before the screening visit with the following exceptions:
5. (a) Patients treated for in situ carcinoma of the cervix who have completed curative therapy and are in remission for at least 12 months prior to signing the informed consent and
6. (b) Patients with basal cell or superficial squamous skin cancer.
7. (c) Patients who have had other malignancies are eligible provided that the patient is in remission and curative therapy was completed at least 5 years prior to the date informed consent was obtained.
9. Diagnosis of systemic mastocytosis.
10. Chronic or ongoing active infections requiring systemic treatment, as well as clinically significant viral, bacterial, or fungal infection within 4 weeks prior to Visit 1.
11. A helminth parasitic infection diagnosed within 24 weeks prior to Visit 1 that has not been treated or has failed to respond to standard of care therapy. A confirmation of a complete resolution of any helminth parasitic infection prior to Visit 1 should be available.
12. A history of known immunodeficiency disorder other than that explained by the use of OCS or other therapy taken for HES. Positive HIV test.
13. Any clinically significant abnormal findings in HES physical examination, vital signs, haematology, or clinical chemistry, during the screening period, which in the opinion of the investigator, may put the patient at risk because of his/her participation in the study or may influence the results of the study or the patient's ability to complete the entire duration of the study.

## Prior/concomitant Therapy

14. Evidence of prior benralizumab treatment failure.
15. Treatment with injectable (SC, IV, or IM) corticosteroids in the 4-week period prior to randomisation.
16. Receipt of any IP within the past 30 days or 5 half-lives, whichever is longer, prior to Visit 1, or current participation in any other interventional clinical study (with the exception of \"registry\"/\"cohort\" trials that may include periodic biological sampling and/or patient questionnaires but in which no other unlicensed IP is administered).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

17. Receipt of any marketed or investigational biologic within 4 months or 5 half-lives prior to the date informed consent is obtained, whichever is longer. Patients on stable therapy for at least 3 months before randomisation who intend to stay on treatment throughout the study with marketed biologics that are not likely to interfere with the assessment of safety and/or efficacy of benralizumab (eg, for the treatment of osteoporosis, migraine, pain, diabetes, obesity, ocular, cardiovascular, or metabolic diseases) can participate in the study.
18. Receipt of live attenuated vaccines 30 days prior to Visit 1.

## Other Exclusions

19. History of hypersensitivity (including anaphylaxis) to any biologic therapy (including benralizumab) or any steroid or steroid-containing product. Known history of allergy or reaction to any component of the IP formulation.
20. Receipt of Ig or blood products within 30 days prior to Visit 1.
21. Patients with a known or suspected history of alcohol or substance abuse at Visit 1, which in the opinion of the investigator, could interfere with the patient's proper completion of the protocol requirements.
22. For women only: Currently pregnant, breastfeeding, or lactating women
5. (a) Both urine and serum pregnancy tests will be done for WOCBP at Visit 1. If serum test is positive, the patient should be excluded.

## Treatments

Participants received the following study treatments (Table 7):

- Benralizumab 30 mg administered via subcutaneous (SC) injection in a 1 mL accessorised prefilled syringe (APFS)
- Placebo administered via SC injection in a 1 mL APFS

It was advised that the site of IP injection be rotated such that the patient receives IP at a different anatomical site at each treatment visit.

The option of self-administration of IP (benralizumab or placebo) was available past Visit 16 (week 54, OLE) only in countries where allowable by local health authorities and ethics committees. If the patient opted for IP self-administration, these visits could be performed remotely (ie, telephone/telemedicine contact); consequently the complete HES physical examination, vital signs, and weight assessments could be skipped.

## Background Medication

Patients were required to be treated with background therapy for HES on a stable dose and regimen for ≥ 4 weeks prior to Visit 1, during the 24-week DB period, and for the first 12 weeks of the OLE treatment period (until Visit 12/Week 36).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Background therapies for HES may have included OCS, immunosuppressive/cytotoxic agents, IFN-α, and other agents used to control HES, and/or manage HES symptoms (eg, topical or nasal steroids, inhaled medications, etc.). Additional medication considered necessary for the patient's safety and well-being, may have been given at the discretion of the Investigator and were recorded.

## Objectives

## Double-blind Period

## Primary:

To evaluate the effect of benralizumab on the time to first HES worsening/flare

## Secondary:

To evaluate the effect of benralizumab on the proportion of patients who experience an HES worsening/flare

To evaluate the effect of benralizumab on the number of HES worsenings/flares

To evaluate the effect of benralizumab on the time to first haematological relapse

To evaluate the effect of benralizumab on patient-reported measure of fatigue

To evaluate the effect of benralizumab on the proportion of patients with haematologic relapse

To evaluate the effect of benralizumab on the number of patients who maintain AEC &lt; 500 cells/μL for 24 weeks

To evaluate the effect of benralizumab on corticosteroid use

To evaluate the effect of benralizumab on health status/HRQoL measures

To evaluate the PK and immunogenicity of benralizumab in patients with HES

## Safety:

To evaluate the safety and tolerability of benralizumab in patients with HES

To evaluate the effect of benralizumab on healthcare resource utilisation due to HES

To evaluate the effect of benralizumab on HES-related hospitalisation

To evaluate the effect of benralizumab on patient-reported measure of HES symptom severity *

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

To characterise the patient-reported experience and treatment benefits of benralizumab through patient interviews *

To evaluate the effect of benralizumab on CRP

To evaluate the effect of benralizumab on biomarkers related to the mechanism of action of benralizumab, eosinophilic inflammation, and HES disease pathogenesis, as well as HES clinical subtypes and baseline predictors of response to benralizumab *

To assess effect of lymphocyte phenotype on responsiveness to benralizumab *

* The following endpoints (except for total IgE and tryptase) related to the objectives above will be reported outside of the CSR: patient interviews to characterise patient-reported experience and treatment benefits (sub-study); HES symptom questionnaire; exploratory biomarkers in serum, nasal secretions, tissue biopsies, and whole blood (for the mechanistic sub-study only); whole blood lymphocyte phenotyping.

## Endpoints Double-blind Period

## Primary

Time to first HES worsening/flare a

- Population b : Full analysis set
- Intercurrent event strategy b : Included in analysis regardless of treatment discontinuation (treatment policy)
- Population-level summary: Hazard ratio

Key: Proportion of patients who experience an HES worsening/flare during the DB treatment period a, c

Key: Number of HES worsenings/flares (annualised rate/year) during the DB period a, c
