<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Serum benralizumab concentrations
- Anti-benralizumab antibodies and nAbs

## Safety:

Safety and tolerability were evaluated in terms of AEs, vital signs, and clinical laboratory assessments.

Assessments related to AEs include:

- Occurrence/frequency
- Relationship to IP as assessed by Investigator
- Intensity
- Seriousness
- Death
- AEs leading to discontinuation of IP
- Other significant AEs

## Exploratory:

- Number of HES-related hospitalisations; length of hospital stay; Intensive care unit days; number of
- HES-related ER visits; number of HES-related outpatient visits (by type); number of HES-related procedures/tests (by specific procedure/test)
- Proportion of HES worsening/flare resulting in hospitalisation
- HES symptom questionnaire
- Patient qualitative interview to characterise patient-reported experience and treatment benefits (sub- study)
- CRP levels

Exploratory biomarkers in:

- Serum
- Plasma
- Urine
- Whole blood (including mechanistic sub-study)
- Tissue biopsies
- Whole blood lymphocyte phenotyping
- a For HES worsening/flare, see the CSP (Appendix 16.1.1, Section 8.2.1).
- b The population and intercurrent event strategy also apply to the secondary endpoints.
- c Key secondary endpoint (multiplicity protected)
- d Haematologic relapse post-baseline: AEC ≥ 1000 cells/μL; baseline is the randomisation AEC

## Sample size

The target patient population was male and female patients 12 years of age and older with documented diagnosis of HES; negative for the FIP1-like-1 (FIP1L1)-platelet-derived growth factor receptor alpha (PDGFRA) fusion tyrosine kinase gene translocation; presence of signs or symptoms of HES worsenings/flares, or ≥ 2 flares within the past 12 months; an absolute eosinophil count (AEC) ≥

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

1000 cells/ μL at screening; and who had been receiving stable background HES regimen for 4 or more weeks before Visit 1.

The study planned to randomise approximately 120 patients 1:1 ratio to receive benralizumab or matching placebo; however, randomisation was permitted to continue until the target number of events was achieved.

The primary analysis was planned to occur when approximately 38 patients had experienced their first HES worsening/flare event during the 24-week DB period and all randomized patients had the opportunity to be followed up for the DB period. Approximately 38 first HES worsening/flare events during the 24-week DB period were required to detect a statistically significant difference between treatment groups at the 2-sided 5% significance level with approximately 82% power if the true treatment effect is a HR of 0.389 (equivalent to 30% of patients on benralizumab experiencing an event by the end of the DB period compared with 60% of patients on placebo assuming exponential survival). An anticipated dropout rate of 5% of patients withdrawing from the study without having flared was factored into the calculation of number of patients needed to be randomised in order to observe 38 flares.

## Randomisation

Patient eligibility for randomisation was determined at Visit 3. Patients were allocated to treatment groups at a 1:1 ratio. The randomisation was stratified by geographic region (North America, Europe, Asia, and Rest of the World) and HES flare status at screening.

Randomisation codes were assigned sequentially in each stratum following patients eligibility for randomization. The code was assigned from a randomisation list prepared by a computerised system (IWRS/IVRS) provided by the MAH.

Patients who failed to meet the inclusion/exclusion criteria were not allowed to be randomised or receive IP.

In case a patient withdrew from the study post-randomisation, the patient's enrolment E-code could not be reused. Withdrawn patients were not replaced.

## Blinding (masking)

The first 24 weeks of this study followed a DB design in which benralizumab and placebo were not visually distinct from each other. All packaging and labelling of the IP were to ensure blinding for all sponsor and investigational site staff. Neither the patient nor any of the investigators or sponsor staff who were involved in the treatment, clinical evaluation, and monitoring of the patients were aware of the treatment received. The site and patients remained blinded after the primary DBL. Open-label IP administration were to begin at Week 24/Visit 9.

While not entirely specific, patients on active benralizumab treatment are expected to have lower eosinophil and basophil counts than patients on placebo. Procedures were in place to mitigate unblinding to WBC counts or biopsy cell counts for each patient from randomisation/baseline (Visit 3/Week 0), during the entire DB treatment period, and for the first 4 weeks of the OLE treatment period (until Visit 10/Week 28).

The randomisation could be interrupted in case of medical emergencies when the appropriate management of the patient required knowledge of the treatment randomisation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The MAH retained the right to break the code in case of unexpected SAEs which were suspected to be causally related to IP and that potentially required expedited reporting to regulatory authorities. Randomisation codes were not to be broken for the planned analyses of data until all decisions on the evaluability of the data from each individual patient have been made and documented.

## Statistical methods

Description of the populations used for analyses

Full Analysis Set (FAS): all patients who were randomised and received at least 1 dose of IP, irrespective of their protocol adherence and continued participation in the study. Patients were analysed according to their randomised treatment irrespective of whether or not they had prematurely discontinued, according to the intent-to-treat principle. Patients who withdrew consent to participate in the study were included up to the date of their study termination. All efficacy analyses were performed using the FAS. The FAS was selected as the primary analysis population, excluding any non-dosed patients, to enable reflection of the true treatment effect. The FAS was considered appropriate as the random assignment to treatment was maintained during exclusion of patients.

All Randomised Patients Analysis Set: all patients who were randomised, irrespective of whether they went on to receive doses of IP and irrespective of their protocol adherence and continued participation in the study. Patients were analysed according to their randomised treatment irrespective of whether or not they had prematurely discontinued, according to the intent-to-treat principle. Patients who had withdrawn consent were included up to the date of their study termination. The All Randomised Patients Analysis Set was used for a sensitivity analysis of the proportion of patients flaring endpoint.

Safety Analysis Set: all patients who received at least 1 dose of IP. Erroneously treated patients during the 24-week DB period (eg, those randomised to benralizumab but actually given placebo) were accounted for in group of the treatment they received. A patient who had on one or several occasions received active IP was classified as active.

PK Analysis Set: all patients who received at least 1 dose of IP and from whom PK blood samples were assumed not to be affected by factors such as protocol violations and who had at least one quantifiable serum PK observation post first dose. All PK summaries were based on this analysis set.

OLE Analysis Set: all patients who entered the OLE part of the study and received at least 1 dose of benralizumab during the OLE treatment period.

The following general principles were applied for the primary analysis:

- The primary estimand for analyses of all efficacy endpoints followed a treatment policy strategy, where all available data captured during the 24-week DB period was included in the analysis, regardless of whether IP was prematurely discontinued or delayed, and irrespective of protocol adherence or changes in background medications.
- The analysis of efficacy endpoints used the FAS, which included all randomised and dosed patients, with patients analysed according to their randomised treatment.
- The analyses of safety data included all available data from the 24-week DB period and exposure and AE data from the OLE period that was available up to the primary analysis DCO. Safety data presentations on the DB period use the Safety Analysis Set and those on the OLE

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

period use the OLE Analysis Set, with patients analysed according to the treatment they received.

- All hypothesis testing was reported using 2-sided tests. The 95% CI was reported for efficacy endpoints as appropriate.
- The last valid value on or prior to the date of randomisation was defined as the baseline measurement for efficacy endpoints while the last valid value prior to first dose of study treatment was defined as the baseline measurement for safety endpoints.

## Testing Strategy to Account for Multiplicity Considerations

The primary analysis used a hierarchical fixed-sequence testing strategy to control the overall Type-I error rate at the 0.05 level, to account for multiplicity for the primary endpoint (time to first HES worsening/flare) and key secondary endpoints.

The primary endpoint was tested at a 2-sided 0.05 significance level:

- If the null hypothesis (i.e., there is no difference in time to first HES worsening/flare between treatment groups) could be rejected with a p-value&lt; 0.05, and time to HES worsening/flare was longer in the benralizumab arm compared with placebo, hierarchical fixed-sequence testing was to continue for the key secondary endpoints at the 2-sided 0.05 level in the order listed in the objectives (Table S1).
- If at any time the null hypothesis could not be rejected at the 2-sided 0.05 level, or if the treatment difference favoured the placebo group compared with the benralizumab arm, further testing for the key secondary endpoints was to stop, and no subsequent null hypothesis was to be rejected.

## Primary Efficacy Analysis Methods

The primary endpoint of the time to first HES worsening/flare was defined as: clinical manifestations or lab abnormalities that resulted in an increase/burst of OCS (prednisone/prednisolone) ≥ 10 mg/day for at least 2 days (other OCSs in equivalent doses permitted), OR an increase or addition of new cytotoxic and/or immunosuppressive therapy, OR hospitalisation.

The stratified log-rank test was used to compare the primary endpoint (time to first HES worsening/flare) between the benralizumab and placebo groups, while adjusting for region.

The Cox proportional hazards model with treatment group and region as covariates was used to estimate the hazard ratio (HR) and its 95% confidence interval (CI). Any tied event times were handled with the Breslow method for the log-rank test and the Efron method for the Cox proportional hazards model. A Kaplan Meier plot of the time to first HES worsening/flare was produced and medians with 95% CIs were estimated if reached.

## Sensitivity Analyses

Three sensitivity analyses for the primary endpoint were conducted:

- An unadjusted analysis (an unstratified log-rank test and a Cox proportional hazards model adjusting for treatment group only).
- A sensitivity analysis which assessed the impact of changes in systemic background therapy before HES worsening/flare.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- An additional sensitivity analysis for the time to HES worsening/flare endpoint, where all patients who withdrew from the 24-week DB period without having flared were considered as a flare event at the time of withdrawal.

## Subgroup Analysis

For each of the subgroup factors in turn, a separate Cox proportional hazards model was fitted including treatment and additional terms for the subgroup effect and the treatment by subgroup interaction to estimate the HR and 95% CI for each level of the subgroup. Subgroup factors would be formally analysed if there were at least 8 total subjects and 1 event within each subgroup level.

A summary of key secondary efficacy analysis methods is provided in Table S2.

## Results

## Participant flow

Approximately 120 eligible patients, including 4 to 6 adolescents (aged 12 to 17) were planned for randomisation. The randomisation was stratified by geographic region and HES flare status at screening. One hundred and thirty-four patients were randomised to treatment during the 24-week DB period, with 67 receiving benralizumab and 67 receiving placebo.

As of the data cut-off (DCO) date of 07 May 2025, 158 patients with HES were enrolled and 134 patients were randomised in 40 study centres across 15 countries. 4/134 patients were adolescents (12-18 years old).

Of the 134 patients randomised, 133 were included in the Full Analysis Set (FAS), and 133 in the Safety Analysis Set. Sixty-seven patients in the benralizumab group were included in the Pharmacokinetic (PK) Analysis Set.

Overall, 127 patients completed the 24-week DB period. A total of 6 (4.5%) patients discontinued IP during the DB period, and discontinuation from IP was low and similar between treatment groups.

## Recruitment

This study was initiated on 20 July 2020 (first subject enrolled) and data cut-off date was 07 May 2025 (last subject, last visit in DB period). The study is currently in the ongoing OLE period to allow treatment with benralizumab targeted for at least 1 year for adults and at least 2 years for adolescents aged ≥ 12 years (to comply with European Medicines Agency Paediatric Committee requirement (EMEA-001214-PIP04-19). Patients were randomized in 40 sites in 15 countries. The majority of patients were recruited from centres in Poland, France, USA, Belgium, and Japan.

## Conduct of the study

All protocol amendments were implemented before unblinding of study data and the rationale for the changes introduced. The most significant of protocol amendments related to:

Modification of inclusion criterion #6 to allow enrolment of those with a history of 2 or more HES worsenings/flares in the preceding 12 months as these patients are at risk of having future HES flares.

Modification of the number of patients required to have a flare for the primary analysis from 47 to approximately 38 flares and to ensure that enough patients were enrolled in order to observe this number of flares reducing the study power to ~ 80% from 90% power originally. In addition, change to the timing of the primary analysis such that the primary analysis would be conducted when all randomised patients have had the opportunity to be followed up for the 24-week DB treatment period and flare target was achieved (Amendment 8).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The overall rate of important protocol deviations was low and balanced between the treatment groups (8 [11.9%] and 10 [15.2%] patients in the benralizumab and placebo groups, respectively). These deviations were not considered to have a meaningful impact on the interpretation of the results.

There were no COVID-19 related important protocol deviations reported.

The most commonly reported important protocol deviation was 'Other' in the benralizumab group and 'Study Assessment' in the placebo group. The category of 'Other' (non-compliance to study protocol other than inclusion/exclusion criteria) includes deviations related to potential unblinding due to local haematology testing, and deviations on missing safety laboratory assessments.

## Baseline data

Demographic data and key baseline characteristics were summarised for each treatment group and the total population using descriptive statistics on FAS. The demographic data and key baseline characteristics included but not limited to the following variables: age (calculated as date of randomisation - date of birth), sex, country, region, race, ethnicity, baseline background medications, baseline eosinophils, HES subtypes, asthma history, organ involvement (primary and nonprimary) etc.

## Demographics

A total of 4 (3.0%) patients were adolescents aged ≥ 12 to &lt; 18 years of age (3 received benralizumab and 1 received placebo) and 12 (9.0%) patients were aged ≥ 18 to ≤ 21 years (6 received benralizumab and 6 received placebo).

## HES Disease History and Baseline Characteristics

The profile of HES disease history was generally similar between groups and reflective of the protocolintended patient population having HES. Both parameters \"Time since first appearance of HES symptoms (years)\" and \"HES diagnosis (years)\" are in general equally distributed treatment groups, though with high variability within groups (duration of symptoms ranging from 0.5 to ~38 years; diagnosis of HES ranging from 0.1 to ~29 years).

Asthma was the most prominent respiratory disease characteristic of patients at study entry as shown in table 14.1.9.2. In general, a balanced diagnosis of asthma was observed in both treatment groups: 34 patients (50.7 %) in benralizumab group and 38 patients (57.6%) in placebo group. In addition, 32 (47.8%) patients in benralizumab group and 35 patients (53%) in placebo group had a diagnosis of asthma at study entry.

## Other Medical and Surgical History

Overall, medical conditions were reported by 116 (87.2%) patients (Table 14.1.7). The most commonly reported medical conditions, excluding common characteristics of HES, were hypertension

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

(26 [19.5%] patients), hypothyroidism (15 [11.3%] patients), and hypercholesterolaemia (11 [8.3%] patients). It is noted that the distribution of patients by SOC and PT for patients with a history of respiratory, thoracic and mediastinal disorders with more prominent the pulmonary manifestations was not balanced between treatment groups, namely a greater proportion of patients were reported in the benralizumab group (13 [19.4%]) compared to patients (7 [10.6%]) in the placebo group.

The most commonly reported surgical histories were appendicectomy (15 [11.3%] patients), cholecystectomy (15 [11.3%] patients), and nasal septal operation (8 [6.0%] patients).

## Concomitant Background HES Medication

Use of permitted concomitant medications was as expected. The majority of patients were receiving background HES therapy at baseline without differences between patients in the benralizumab and placebo groups (Table 12). Of note in patients receiving systemic OCS at baseline, despite low numbers, a higher proportion of patients were receiving prednisone &gt;10-&lt; 20 mg/d in the placebo group 9 (13.6%) compared with 4 6.0%) in the benralizumab group. In patients receiving oral corticosteroids (OCS) at baseline (102 [76.7%] patients), the median OCS dose was 5.00 mg/day. A total of 11 (8.3%) patients were on a cytotoxic or immunosuppressive therapy. Similar proportions of patients in both groups were receiving OCS and cytotoxic/immunosuppressive therapy. For patients receiving background OCS, the mean daily dose was similar between treatment groups.

## Changes in Background HES Therapy

During the DB period, 23 (34.3%) patients in the benralizumab group and 34 (51.5%) patients in the placebo group changed background HES therapy (Table 14.1.10.6). Assessment of changes in background HES therapy focused on corticosteroids, cytotoxic, or immunosuppressive therapies. A change could include increases (increases in daily dose or initiation of a new therapy) or decreases (reduction in daily dose or stopping a therapy).

A lower proportion of patients in the benralizumab group required an increase in background therapy than in the placebo group (18 [26.9%] versus 33 [50.0%] patients, respectively).

## Non-permitted Concomitant Medication

In addition, the use of non-permitted (restricted and prohibited) concomitant medications was low and similar between patients in the benralizumab and placebo groups, both by ATC classification and generic term. During the DB period, non-permitted concomitant medications were taken by 4 (6.0%) patients in the benralizumab group and 3 (4.5%) patients in the placebo group. Confirmed cases of prohibited medication use were reported as important protocol deviations.

## Treatment Compliance

Overall mean compliance to treatment was high and similar between groups. For the FAS, mean (SD) compliance was 100% for the benralizumab group and 98.6% (4.5%) for the placebo group.

[a] All randomised patients analysis set includes all patients who are randomised irrespective of whether they go on to receive doses of IP and irrespective of their protocol adherence and continued participation in the study.

[b] Full analysis set includes all patients who are randomised and receive at least 1 dose of IP, irrespective of their protocol adherence and continued participation in the study.

[c] Safety analysis set includes all patients who receive at least 1 dose of IP.

[d] PK analysis set includes all patients who receive at least 1 dose of IP and from whom PK blood samples are assumed not to be affected by factors such as protocol violations and who had at least one quantifiable serum PK observation post first dose.

One patient was excluded from the FAS: the patient was randomised but did not receive any treatment due to issue related to IWRS. The patient was registered as randomised in IWRS, however, the patient withdrew consent before the next visit and left the study without receiving any treatment.

## Outcomes and estimation

## Primary Endpoint:

Time to First HES Worsening/Flare

Treatment with benralizumab delayed the time to first HES worsening/flare compared with placebo as shown by the Kaplan Meier plot in Figure 2 and resulted in a statistically significant 65% reduction in the risk of first HES worsening/flare compared with placebo in the DB period (HR 0.35 [95% CI: 0.18 to 0.69], p = 0.0024) (Table 13).

## Key Secondary Endpoints

## Proportion of Patients Who Experienced a HES Worsening/Flare

Statistically significantly fewer patients treated with benralizumab experienced a HES worsening/flare compared with placebo; the proportion of patients with a HES worsening/flare was 22.4% (15 patients) in the benralizumab group versus 45.5% (30 patients) in the placebo group (CMH test primary analysis p = 0.003) (Table 14), representing a 52% relative reduction in the proportion of patients flaring. In this analysis, any patients who withdrew from the study without having flared were included in the analysis as if they had flared.

## Number of HES Worsenings/Flares (Annualised Rate)

Treatment with benralizumab resulted in significantly fewer HES worsenings/flares compared with the placebo group (annualised rate 0.41 versus 1.23) and a statistically significant 66% reduction in the annualised rate of HES worsenings/flares (rate ratio 0.34, 95% CI: 0.18 to 0.63, p &lt; 0.001) (Table 15).

Time to First Haematologic Relapse (AEC ≥ 1000 Cells/μL)

Treatment with benralizumab delayed the time to first haematologic relapse (Figure 4) and resulted in a statistically significant 92% reduction in the risk of first haematologic relapse compared with placebo (HR 0.08 [95% CI: 0.03 to 0.20], p &lt; 0.0001) (Table 17).

Haematologic relapse was defined in the CSP, as an AEC of ≥ 1000 cells/μL at any post-randomisation visit, based on central laboratory assessment, and was included as an endpoint as it is an objective laboratory measure which signals increasing disease severity. The cut-off was chosen, based on the inclusion criterion of AEC &lt; 1000 cells/μL following local laboratory assessment (post 2-day course of OCS). An increase in AEC to ≥ 1000 cells/μL is 10-fold above normal physiological AEC levels and, in patients with HES, subsequently leads to clinical symptoms of hypereosinophilia (Roufosse et al 2022a, Roufosse et al 2022b); therefore, delaying or preventing haematologic relapse is of clinical relevance to patients with HES.

The Kaplan Meier plot showed early and clear separation between treatment groups (Figure 4).

## Fatigue (PROMIS Fatigue Short Form 7a) at Week 24

The PROMIS measurement system (PROMIS Health Measures) consists of measures of physical, mental, and social health that can be used in the general population and in individuals with chronic conditions. A cognitive debriefing study consisting of interviews with 15 adults with HES in the US confirmed that the 7-item short form of the PROMIS Fatigue instrument is appropriate for measuring fatigue in this patient population. The items were comprehensible to patients, broadly covered aspects of fatigue that mattered to patients, and had appropriate and interpretable response options. From a psychometric perspective, PROMIS T-scores are easily interpretable and comparable across populations and studies. The 7-item short form reduces patient burden while preserving precision. Anchor-based analyses (using PGI-S as the anchor) were performed prior to unblinding to determine whether the observed changes from baseline in PROMIS could be considered meaningful to patients.

Treatment with benralizumab statistically significantly reduced fatigue compared with placebo at Week 24. The LS mean (95% CI) absolute change from baseline in standardised total score at Week 24 was - 8.6 (-10.6 to -6.6) in the benralizumab group and -3.9 (-6.0 to -1.8) in the placebo group at Week 24, and the LS mean difference between groups was -4.72 (95% CI: -7.6 to -1.8; p = 0.0017) (Table 14.2.8.2).

A greater improvement in fatigue was observed with benralizumab compared with placebo at Week 4 (nominal p = 0.0092) and was maintained to Week 24 (Figure 5 and Table 14.2.8.2).

The results of anchor-based analyses that incorporated the patients' perception of improvement indicated that the observed reduction in fatigue is representative of a meaningful within-patient improvement. There was reasonable correlation between change in PROMIS fatigue score and change in PGI-S at Week 24 (Spearmans correlation coefficient of 0.547) supporting the use of the PGI-S variable as an anchor (Table 14.2.8.5).

The changes in PROMIS scores were anchored to the change in PGI-S scores at Week 24, pooled across treatment groups, to define ranges of change in PROMIS thresholds considered to represent meaningful within-patient improvements. The study planned to use a 1-category improvement on the PGI-S scale as the primary anchor for assessing change in PROMIS scores, and to explore the separation in PROMIS scores across the change in PGI-S categories based on eCDF and ePDF plots. In

0.547

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

a review of blinded data prior to DBL, some separation of groups was observed for the 1-category improvement versus the no change group; however, more distinct separation was observed for the 2category improvement group. Therefore, to explore the range of thresholds that may be considered meaningful, thresholds of 6 and 13 points were pre-defined to align with mean and median changes in the 1- and 2-category improvement groups, respectively. The final eCDF and ePDF plots confirmed the assessment made on blinded data.

For both the 6- and 13-point meaningful change thresholds, more patients treated with benralizumab achieved a meaningful reduction from baseline in fatigue severity score at Week 24 compared with placebo (33 [49.3%] patients versus 22 [33.3%] patients for the 6-point threshold, 21 [31.3%] patients versus 7 [10.6%] patients for the 13-point threshold for benralizumab versus placebo, respectively) (Table 14.2.8.8).

The proportion of patients who have a haematologic relapse or withdraw from the study over the DB treatment period is defined as the number of patients who have a first haematologic relapse or withdraw prior to completing in the DB period divided by the number of patients in the the analysis. Haematologic relapse is defined when Absolute Eosinophil Count (AEC) post baseline is greater than or equal to 1,000 cells/U1 for the first time.

DB period: starts from the date of randomisation until the earliest date of (date of the first of Benra open label dose, study day 183, date of last contact, and data cut-off date).

[a] The pooled percentage difference and 95% CI is based on Mantel-Haenszel weight adjusted by region.

[b] Odds ratio, its 95% CI and the associated p-value are estimated using a logistic regression model including covariates for treatment and region. Odds ratio lower than 1 favours Benra.

## Proportion of Patients Who Maintain AEC &lt; 500 Cells/μL for 24 Weeks

More patients treated with benralizumab achieved and maintained AEC &lt; 500 cells/μL compared with placebo; the proportion of patients with AEC &lt; 500 cells/μL was 91.0% (61 patients) in the benralizumab group versus 12.1% (8 patients) in the placebo group (Table 19).

Proportion of Patients Who Require an Increase in Corticosteroids from Baseline

Increasing corticosteroids from baseline includes patients increasing OCS dose for those already on OCS at baseline, or patients starting any new or additional course of systemic corticosteroids given for HES. Medical review confirmed that only changes in medications related to the disease under study were included in this analysis.

Fewer patients treated with benralizumab (17 [25.4%] patients) required an increase in corticosteroids compared with placebo (32 [48.5%] patients) (Table 20).

Benra = Benralizumab. DB = Double blind. CI = Confidence interval. N = Number of patients in treatment group. SCS = Systemic Corticosteroids.

Includes any increase of at least 1 mg Prednisone equivalent versus the previous day where reason for therapy is DUS/HES flare or for another condition with closely related symptoms.

Source: Table 14.2.7.1

The mean (SD) number of times a patient required a systemic corticosteroid increase was lower in the benralizumab group (0.3 [0.58] patients) than the placebo group (0.6 [0.76] patients). A higher proportion of patients who received benralizumab (50 [74.6%] patients) had zero increases in corticosteroids compared with placebo (34 [51.5%] patients).

The total cumulative OCS dose was lower in the benralizumab group (LS mean 876.3 mg) than the placebo group (LS mean 1221.2 mg) (LS mean difference -344.93 mg [95% CI: -637.6 to -52.3])

In addition, when including corticosteroid, cytotoxic or immunosuppressive background HES therapies, a lower proportion of patients in the benralizumab group (26.9% [18 patients]) required an increase in background therapy than in the placebo group (50.0% [33 patients]).

Improvements were observed in patients treated with benralizumab in both the PCS and MCS components of the SF-36v2.

- PCS: LS mean difference in change from baseline to Week 24 for benralizumab versus placebo was 4.54 (95% CI: 1.9 to 7.1, nominal p = 0.0008)
- MCS: LS mean difference in change from baseline to Week 24 for benralizumab versus placebo was 2.60 (95% CI: -0.4 to 5.6, nominal p = 0.0852).

Improvements in PCS and MCS in patients treated with benralizumab were observed at Week 12 (nominal p = 0.0104 and 0.0026, respectively) and were sustained to Week 24 (nominal p = 0.0008 and 0.0852, respectively)

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The proportion of patients achieving a response in the PCS (≥ 3.4-unit increase) and MCS (≥ 4.6-unit increase) at Week 24 numerically favoured benralizumab over placebo (Table 14.2.9.5):

- PCS: 37 (55.2%) and 25 (37.9%) patients in the benralizumab and placebo groups, respectively
- MCS: 30 (44.8%) and 20 (30.3%) patients in the benralizumab and placebo groups, respectively

Improvements from baseline to Weeks 12 and 24 (nominal p &lt; 0.05) were also observed in patients treated with benralizumab in the bodily pain (BP), general health perceptions (GH), and vitality (VT) components.

## PGI-S and PGI-C

At Week 24, the most frequent PGI-S categories in the benralizumab group were 'no symptoms' (17 [26.6%] patients) and 'very mild' (17 [26.6%] patients), whereas the most frequent PGI-S categories in the placebo group were 'very mild' (16 [27.1%] patients) and 'mild' (14 [23.7%] patients).

At Week 24, a greater proportion of patients in the benralizumab group (27 [42.2%] patients) than the placebo group (14 [24.1%] patients) had a PGI-C response of 'much better'.

## Exploratory Endpoints

Patients in benralizumab group demonstrated improvement in parameters relevant to quality of life (Health Care Resource Utilisation; Proportion of HES Worsenings/flares Resulting in Hospitalisation; HES Symptom Questionnaire; Spirometry).

## Analysis performed across trials (pooled analyses and meta-analysis)

Not applicable

## Clinical studies in special populations

## Subgroup Analyses

The consistency of benralizumab treatment effect was investigated for the primary efficacy endpoint Time to first HES worsening/flare in the following subgroups: age, sex, race, and geographic region.

The uniformity of the detected overall treatment effect on the primary efficacy variable was tested using exploratory subgroup analyses on patients from the full analysis set. The time to first HES worsening/flare is calculated as: start date of the first HES worsening - date of randomisation + 1. For patients who do not experience a HES worsening/flare, the time to first HES worsening/flare is right censored at the end of the DB period corresponding to the earliest date of (date of the first of Benra open label dose, study day 183, date of last contact, and data cut-off date). The analysis was performed using a Cox proportional hazards model including treatment group, the subgroup modality and the interaction between treatment group and the subgroup modality tested as covariates. Hazard ratio less than 1 indicated a lower rate of incidence for patients on Benra. Median baseline blood eosinophil values were equal to 115 cells/uL.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The treatment effect observed for benralizumab over placebo in the overall population was consistent across all pre-defined subgroups (Figure 3).

The study was not sized for any of the individual subgroup evaluations and no adjustments were made for multiple testing in subgroup analyses, hence these subgroup analyses should be interpreted with caution due to lower number of patients and events across the individual subgroups, which leads to greater uncertainty in their point estimates, and wider CI.

Based on the proposed indication the target of benralizumab are adults and adolescents with inadequately controlled HES syndrome; therefore results from the analysis of efficacy in the age group of 12-18 years old patients would be expected; however, due to limited number of enrolled patients from this age group in NATRON (three adolescents were randomised to the benralizumab group and one to the placebo group) the MAH reported the primary efficacy results from the extended subgroup of patients aged ≤ 21 years (n=16); It should be noted that the proportion of patients with HES flare/worsening by week 12 was higher in the benralizumab group compared to the placebo group. However, two out of nine (22.2%) patients in the benralizumab group and 3/7 (42.9%) patients in the placebo group &lt; 21 years of age experienced a HES worsening/flare during the DB period consistent with the results from the overall study population.

## 2.4.3. Discussion on clinical efficacy

The proposed extension of indication to include treatment of adults and adolescents with hypereosinophilic syndrome (HES) for FASENRA is based on a single Phase III, randomised, DB, multicentre, parallel-group, placebo-controlled study (D3254C00001; NATRON).

## Design and conduct of clinical studies

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

NATRON is an ongoing multicentre, randomised, double-blind, parallel-group, placebo-controlled, 24week phase III study with an open-label extension period to evaluate the efficacy and safety of benralizumab 30 mg SC Q4W in patients with HES.

Despite recruitment challenges that reduced the planned number of flares and power modestly, the trial design and primary analysis were maintained and are considered interpretable. Key concerns on deficiencies in documentation and uniformity of HES diagnosis and HES flare assessment (no consensus physician-documented criteria for organ manifestations) have been addressed by the MAH: inconsistencies in AEC results between central and local laboratories were attributed to variations in the temperature of blood samples and delays in their analysis; it was clarified that the number of available biopsies in NATRON was insufficient to support firm conclusions or inform whether blood hypereosinophilia was accompanied by tissue hypereosinophilia. Furthermore, the MAH indicated that radiographic evidence for identification of organ involvement was not available; both was considered acceptable by the CHMP. Moreover, it was confirmed that all randomised patients were responsive to anti-inflammatory therapy which highlights corticosteroid responsiveness as a key characteristic of the target population.

The study was designed with a 24-week, DB, placebo-controlled period followed by an OLE period of at least 1 year for adults and 2 years for adolescents aged ≥ 12 years. During the DB period of the study, patients were randomised to receive either benralizumab or placebo in addition to prior stable HES background therapy with the aim to compare the efficacy and safety of benralizumab to placebo. Patients which had completed the 24-week DB period on IP were eligible to enter OLE treatment period where benralizumab was exclusively administered to all patients for the assessment of its long-term efficacy, safety, and tolerability in patients with HES. Due to recruitment challenges during the study caused by the low prevalence of HES, the number of flares required to achieve the primary analysis was reduced from 47 to 38, prior to unblinding for the primary analysis. The reduced flare target still allowed high power (~ 80% [reduced from 90% power originally]) for statistical significance to be demonstrated under the assumptions outlined in the sample size justification and was therefore not considered to have compromised the integrity of the trial or validity of the results. The primary database lock (DBL) was planned to occur when approximately 38 patients had their first HES worsening/flare event during the DB treatment period and all randomised patients had the opportunity to be followed up for the 24-week DB treatment period. The final DBL will occur after the last patient completes the OLE period.

Approximately 120 eligible patients were planned to be randomised at a 1:1 ratio to receive either benralizumab 30 mg or matching placebo Q4W. Approximately 4 to 6 adolescent patients (aged 12 to 17 years) were expected to be randomised. The randomisation was stratified by geographic region (North America, Europe, Asia, and Rest of the World) and HES flare status at screening.

The target population of the study was adults and adolescents aged ≥ 12 years, with the following key inclusion criteria:

- Documented diagnosis of HES (history of persistent eosinophilia &gt; 1500 cells/μL without secondary cause on 2 examinations [interval ≥ 1 month; Valent et al 2012] and evidence of end organ manifestations attributable to eosinophilia).
- Documented negative testing for the FIP1L1-PDGFRA fusion tyrosine kinase gene translocation.
- Stable HES treatment dose(s) and regimen for ≥ 4 weeks at the time of Visit 1.
- Signs or symptoms of HES worsening/flare and/or laboratory abnormalities indicative of HES worsening/flare (other than isolated eosinophilia) at Visit 1 or a documented history of 2 or more HES worsenings/flares within 12 months prior to Visit 1 requiring an escalation in therapy.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Screening AEC ≥ 1000 cells/μL at Visit 1 (assessed by local laboratory).
- Corticosteroid responsiveness defined as an AEC &lt; 1000 cells/μL after a 2-day course of OCS (prednisone/prednisolone) 1 mg/kg/day at Visit 2 (assessed by local laboratory). Other OCSs in equivalent doses were permitted.

Patients were excluded if they had:

- Life-threatening HES and/or HES complication(s) as judged by the Investigator.
- Presence of FIP1L1-PDGFRA fusion tyrosine kinase gene translocation or other known imatinibsensitive mutation.
- Definitive diagnosis of EGPA.
- Hypereosinophilia of unknown significance.
- Current or history of malignancy within 5 years before the screening visit.
- Evidence of prior benralizumab treatment failure.
- Treatment with injectable (SC, intravenous, or intramuscular) corticosteroids in the 4-week period prior to randomization.

Several issues pertaining to the inclusion criteria for documenting HES and HES flare in the context of updated diagnosis and classification of HES and related disorders have been addressed by the MAH: more specifically, the characterization of HES at baseline and monitoring of changes during the DB treatment period would be expected to rely upon a consensus of physician-documented changes in clinical signs and symptoms of patients experiencing HES worsening/flare. However, there is no such element in the documents in support of the application. Such a deficiency leaves a gap on the uniformity of assessment of end organ clinical manifestations. In the MAH's response it was clarified that the diagnosis of HES and organ involvement met ICOG-EO criteria. Consequently, all participants were presumed to have tissue hypereosinophilia-related organ manifestations. In addition, compliance with protocol defined inclusion criteria was validated further through source data verification incl. medical records, though detailed outcomes of diagnosis were not reported in assessments. With regards to uncertainties on the documentation of tissue eosinophilia in the NATRON study population, the MAH submitted skin biopsy images from 2 patients using H&amp;E and immunohistochemistry staining at screening to demonstrate the presence of intact eosinophils and their degranulation components in dermal tissue. The MAH acknowledged that the limited number of biopsies obtained in the study and the elevated levels of serum eosinophil granule proteins do not provide confirmatory evidence of the presence of tissue hypereosinophilia in the study population

The primary efficacy endpoint in NATRON was defined as the time to first HES worsening/flare during the DB period.

The key secondary endpoints (multiplicity protected) were:

- Proportion of patients who experienced a HES worsening/flare during the DB period.
- Number of HES worsenings/flares (annualised rate) during the DB period.
- Time to first haematologic relapse (AEC ≥ 1000 cells/μL) during the DB period.
- Change from baseline in PROMIS Fatigue Short Form 7a score at Week 24.

HES is a rare, debilitating disease; thus, a formal dose-ranging study has not been performed by the MAH. The rationale for a single-study clinical development programme evaluating a single benralizumab dosing regimen of 30 mg SC Q4W in HES is supported by the higher eosinophil burden of HES compared to asthma, the PK profile and PD effects of eosinophil depletion in blood and tissues, the results of a Phase II Investigator confined by the small size and the inclusion of patients with clinical characteristics overlapping with other eosinophilic disorders, the extensive safety database of the MAH and the recent approval of the same dosing regimen as add on treatment for relapsing or refractory EGPA based on the results of the MANDARA study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Efficacy data and additional analyses

As of the data cut-off (DCO) date of 07 May 2025, 158 patients with HES were enrolled and 134 patients were randomised in 40 study centres across 15 countries. 4/134 patients were adolescents (12-17 years old). One hundred and thirty-four patients were randomised to treatment during the 24week DB period, with 67 allocated to benralizumab and 67 allocated to placebo. One hundred and thirty-three patients were included in the Full Analysis Set (FAS). The analysis of efficacy endpoints included all data captured during the 24-week DB period using the FAS. Overall, 127 [94.8%) patients completed the 24-week DB period: 65 [97.0%] and 62 [92.5%] patients in the benralizumab and placebo groups, respectively. A low rate of discontinuation was observed: a total of 6 (4.5%) patients discontinued IP during the DB period.

The primary endpoint of the time to first HES worsening/flare was compared between the benralizumab and placebo groups using a log-rank test stratified by region. The HR and its 95% CI were estimated using a Cox proportional hazards model with treatment group and region as covariates. It was demonstrated that treatment with benralizumab resulted in a statistically and clinically significant 65% reduction in the risk of first worsening/flare compared with placebo (HR 0.35 [95% CI: 0.18 to 0.69], p = 0.0024). The impact of changes in systemic background therapy and the impact of missing data from patients who withdrew from the DB period of the study without having flared were explored in sensitivity analyses of the primary endpoint which correlated with the results from the primary analysis. The treatment effect observed for benralizumab over placebo in the overall population was consistent across pre-defined subgroups, albeit these subgroup analyses should be interpreted with caution due to lower number of patients and events across the individual subgroups, which leads to greater uncertainty in their point estimates.

Moreover, the efficacy of benralizumab was supported by results from the key secondary endpoints:

- proportion of patients who had HES worsenings/flares with statistically significantly fewer patients treated with benralizumab 22.4% (15 patients) experiencing a HES worsening/flare compared with placebo 45.5% (30 patients)
- the number of HES worsenings/flares (annualised rate), with statistically significantly fewer HES worsenings/flares compared with the placebo group (annualised rate 0.41 versus 1.23) and a statistically significant 66% reduction in the annualised rate of HES worsenings/flares (rate ratio 0.34, 95% CI: 0.18 - 0.63)
- time to haematologic relapse, where treatment with benralizumab delayed the time to first haematologic relapse and resulted in a statistically significant reduction (92%) in the risk of first haematologic relapse compared with placebo (HR 0.08 [95% CI: 0.03 to 0.20],
- change in fatigue (as assessed by PROMIS score) where the treatment with benralizumab deomonstrated a statistically significantly reduced fatigue compared to placebo at Week 24, as measured by the PROMIS Fatigue Short Form 7a

Similarly, patients in benralizumab group demonstrated improved control on background therapy compared to placebo group as regards increase in OCS, systemic corticosteroids, cytotoxic, and immunosuppressive medication and improvement in parameters relevant to quality of life (Health Care Resource Utilisation; Proportion of HES Worsenings/flares Resulting in Hospitalisation; HES Symptom Questionnaire; Spirometry).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Assessment of paediatric data on clinical efficacy

Based on the wording of the proposed indication, the target patient group are adults and adolescents with inadequately controlled HES. Due to low recruitment of patients 12-18 years of age in NATRON (only three adolescents were randomised to the benralizumab group and one to the placebo group), the MAH reported the efficacy results from an extended subgroup of patients aged ≤ 21 years (n=16) to meet the requirement (a minimum number of at least eight patients and 1 event within each subgroup) for a formal analysis of a subgroup factor level per the Statistical methods-Primary Efficacy Analysis Methods. Two out of nine (22.2%) patients in the benralizumab group and 3/7 (42.9%) patients in the placebo group &lt; 21 years of age experienced a HES worsening/flare during the DB period consistent with the results from the overall study population. Of note the proportion of patients &lt;=21 with HES flare/worsening by week 12 was higher in the benralizumab group compared to the placebo group in patients &lt;21 years of age.

The MAH stated that efficacy data from patients aged &lt;=21 years could be considered as supportive given the physiological similarities of these young adults to adolescent patients. In the publication by Williams et al., 2016 it is reported that clinical manifestations were similar between children and adults, with the exception of pulmonary involvement (excluding asthma), which was more frequent in adults, and gastrointestinal involvement being more frequent in children.

With respect to the age group of interest (12-17 years old patients with HES), 1/3 adolescents who received benralizumab experienced a HES worsening/flare. In addition, the only adolescent in the placebo group also experienced a HES worsening/flare. No meaningful conclusions on the treatment effect of benralizumab in adolescent patients with HES could be drawn from the submitted results.

As a consequence, the MAH was requested to discuss if extrapolation of efficacy/safety from adults to adolescents based on 'ICH guideline E11A on paediatric extrapolation - Scientific guideline (EMA/CHMP/ICH/205218/2022)' would be possible and provide relevant supportive analyses including, a sensitivity analysis, comparing the exposure simulations using the current PopPK model and a model updated with fixed allometric exponents.

The MAH repeated the fixed exponent PopPK simulations applying 0.75 value for both clearance parameters (CL/F and Q/F) and a uniform bodyweight distribution from 30 kg. In addition, exposure was stratified on bodyweight. Based on the submitted PopPK modeling data which covered a range of 30 to 110 kg, the adolescents at the lower body weight (30-40 kg) exhibited higher exposures. The MAH performed an extrapolation assessment with the objective to evaluate whether efficacy and safety data for benralizumab in adults can be applied to adolescents with HES, in accordance with ICH E11A. The MAH discussed available evidence to support the similarity between adult and paediatric HES on pathophysiology, diagnostic criteria, clinical presentation and disease phenotype. The MAH also provided information from RWE data on the similarity of the overall pattern of organ involvement between adults and adolescents with HES, although with pronounced differences in frequency. The limited approved therapeutic options for paediatric patients with HES (systemic corticosteroids as first-line therapy) with recognised long-term toxicity were discussed. Additionaly, the MAH submitted the results from the analysis of real-world data in adults and adolescents treated with mepolizumab (IL-5-targeted therapy) showing comparable haematological remission rates. The extrapolation exercise is considered satisfactory to support efficacy in adolescents from 12 years of age (for the restriction on weight please refer to the safety discussion).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The collection of additional data planned by the MAH to address the main uncertainty, namely the very limited number of adolescents exposed to benralizumab in NATRON (n=4) is endorsed: long-term follow-up through NATRON open-label extension (OLE) with analysis data from 12-52 months of exposure, long-term safety data from the open-label ROSY-F rollover study (for participants who have completed a previous study with benralizumab) and a paediatric basket study (CLIPS) aiming to enrol ≥14 paediatric HES and EGPA patients aged 6-&lt;18 years to inform the PK, PD, safety and efficacy of benralizumab in adolescents with HES and EGPA.

## 2.4.4. Conclusions on the clinical efficacy

Based on the pivotal efficacy data from the NATRON study, benralizumab demonstrated consistent efficacy over placebo in patients with HES during the 24-week double-blind period: significant reduction was demonstrated in the risk of first HES worsening/flare by 65% (HR 0.35), in the proportion of patients experiencing a flare (22.4% vs 45.5%), and the annualised flare rate by 66% (rate ratio 0.34); a delayed haematologic relapse with a 92% reduction in risk (HR 0.08) was reported in patients treated with benralizumab, as well as, a statistically significant improvement in fatigue (PROMIS Fatigue Short Form 7a) at Week 24. Moreover, improvement was reported on the control of background therapy and several quality-of-life and healthcare-utilization measures; treatment effects were consistent across predefined subgroups though subgroup estimates carry greater uncertainty due to small numbers. Very few adolescent patients aged 12 to 17 were enrolled in the NATRON study but efficacy is supported based on extrapolation. Efficacy was considered demonstrated for adults with HES and adolescents weighing at least 35 kg (please refer also to safety discussion), based on extrapolation, in accordance with ICH E11A.

## 2.5. Clinical safety

## Introduction

The primary evidence for safety of benralizumab in HES is based on key safety data from the primary analysis of NATRON during the 24-week DB period and exposure and AE data from the OLE period that was available up to the primary analysis DCO date of 07 May 2025. The primary analysis DCO occurred when 41 flares had been observed in the DB period and all randomised patients had the opportunity to be followed up for the 24-week DB period.

The safety results from NATRON are supported by the safety and tolerability of benralizumab documented in the original submission of benralizumab for the treatment of severe eosinophilic asthma from long-term pivotal studies (SIROCCO [D3250C00017], CALIMA [D3250C00018], and BORA [D3250C00021]), and EGPA, from the Phase III study MANDARA [D3253C00001], where the Q4W dosing regimen was used. In addition, available post-marketing data complemented the findings from the safety studies.

SIROCCO and CALIMA were Phase III, global, multicentre, randomised, DB, parallel-group, placebocontrolled studies designed to evaluate the efficacy, safety, PK, and immunogenicity of a fixed 30 mg dose of benralizumab administered SC in Q4W or Q8W dosing regimens for 48 and 56 weeks, respectively, in patients with a history of asthma exacerbations and severe eosinophilic asthma. Data was available for 841 patients receiving benralizumab Q4W and 822 patients receiving benralizumab Q8W. The results demonstrated that both Q4W and Q8W dosing regimens were well tolerated and had consistent safety profiles. The most frequently reported ADRs across the studies were headache, pharyngitis, pyrexia, injection site reactions, and hypersensitivity reactions; all were reported at a frequency of (≥ 1/100 to &lt; 1/10). The safety profile of benralizumab 30 mg in both Q4W and Q8W dosing regimens was also supported by BORA a 56-week, blinded, randomised, parallel-group extension study in patients who completed the predecessor studies SIROCCO and CALIMA. A total of 783 and 793 patients were enrolled in the Q4W and Q8W cohorts of BORA, respectively.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

MANDARA was a 52-week, DB, randomised, active-controlled, parallel-group study in patients with relapsing or refractory EGPA. A total of 70 patients were randomised to receive benralizumab 30 mg Q4W. The safety profile during the 52-week DB period was consistent with the asthma trials. The frequency of ADRs was similar to those reported in asthma, with the exception of headache, which occurred in 17% of benralizumab treated patients with EGPA compared with 8% in patients with asthma. No additional ADRs were identified.

Safety and tolerability of benralizumab in patients with HES were evaluated in terms of AEs, vital signs, and clinical laboratory assessments. Assessment of frequency, relationship to IP, intensity, seriousness, fatal events, AEs leading to discontinuation of IP and other significant AEs were included in the evaluation of AEs. All safety analyses for the DB period of the NATRON study were performed using the Safety Analysis Set, which included all patients who received at least 1 dose of IP. All safety analyses for the OLE period were performed using the OLE Analysis Set, which included all patients who entered the OLE part of the study and who received at least 1 dose of benralizumab during this period.

## Patient exposure

The analysis for the duration of exposure to the IP included data captured during the DB and OLE periods through the DCO date. By the NATRON primary analysis DCO 126 patients had received at least one dose of IP in the OLE period and were included in the OLE Analysis Set. Safety findings from the OLE period were summarised for patients who received benralizumab 30 mg Q4W in the DB and OLE periods (referred to as 'benralizumab group'), and patients who received placebo during the DB period followed by benralizumab 30 mg Q4W during the OLE period (referred to as 'placebo switched to benralizumab group').

## DB Period

The mean duration of IP administration during the DB period was similar between the benralizumab group (140.4 days) and the placebo (138.1 days) treatment groups in line with the balanced patient disposition during the DB period (Table 24).

## OLE Period

At the time of primary analysis DCO, patients had a range of exposures to benralizumab, with the earliest randomised patients being treated with benralizumab for up to 4.3 years within the OLE period. Benralizumab exposure during the OLE period was similar between patients previously randomised to benralizumab and those previously randomised to placebo (Table 25). The median duration of administration of benralizumab during the ongoing OLE period was 595 days (approx. 1.63 years) in the benralizumab group and 586 days (approx. 1.6 years) in the placebo switched to benralizumab group.

## Adverse events

## DB Period

There was no clinically meaningful difference in the event rates of AEs and SAEs with onset during the DB period between the benralizumab group and the placebo group (Table 26). SAEs and AEs leading to discontinuation of IP were reported by a low number of patients from both treatment groups. The proportion of patients with an AE possibly related to IP by the Investigator was higher in the benralizumab group (11 [16.4%] patients) than in the placebo group (5 [7.6%] patients). Several of the related AEs are known ADRs for benralizumab and are included in current prescribing information. One patient in the benralizumab group had an AE with outcome of death during the DB period (for more information see below subsection 'Deaths Serious Adverse Events, Discontinuation of Investigational Product Due to Adverse Events, and Other Significant Adverse Events').

## DB Period

## Adverse events by relatedness:

The investigator assessed relatedness for each AE, based on a reasonable possibility the AE was caused by the IP (\"possibly related\").

The majority of AEs during the DB period were assessed as \"not possibly related to IP\" by the Investigator. A higher proportion of patients in the benralizumab group (16.4%) than in the placebo group (7.6%) reported AEs during the DB period that were assessed as possibly related to IP by the Investigator (Table 29). General disorders and administration site conditions related to IP were reported by 10.4% of patients in the benralizumab group compared to 1.5% in the placebo group; several of the related AEs are known ADRs for benralizumab.

Relatedness of malignancy events, helminth infections, hypersensitivity reactions, and injection site reactions is displayed in section Adverse Events of Interest.

## Adverse events by intensity:

The majority of AEs assessed by maximum reported intensity during the DB period were characterised as moderate in intensity by the Investigator. No clinically meaningful difference was observed in the proportion of patients with severe AEs during the DB period in the benralizumab group (6 [9.0%] patients) compared with the placebo group (5 [7.6%] patients).

There were no severe AEs that occurred in more than 1 patient in either group. The majority of AEs during the DB period were transient in nature.

## OLE Period

Among the 126 patients in the OLE period, the most common AEs by PT reported in the benralizumab group (with corresponding numbers for the placebo switched to benralizumab group) were (on descending frequency): upper respiratory tract infection (15 [23.1%] and 15 [24.6%] patients, respectively), COVID-19 (12 [18.5%] and 13 [21.3%] patients, respectively), nasopharyngitis (9 [13.8%] and 6 [9.8%] patients, respectively) and gastroenteritis (5 [7.7%] and 6 [9.8%] patients, respectively).

Overall, the pattern of reported AEs in the OLE period was similar to those reported for benralizumab during the DB period.

## Adverse Events of Interest

## Malignancy

One SAE of malignancy (chronic eosinophilic leukaemia) was reported in a patient (1.5%) in the benralizumab group during the DB period. The event was considered moderate in intensity by the Investigator and not related to the IP. The patient was discontinued from the study (severe noncompliance with the protocol).

Two (3.1%) patients in the benralizumab group reported nonserious AEs of malignancy (unconfirmed T-cell lymphoma and prostate cancer) during the OLE period. It should be noted that the event of unconfirmed T-cell lymphoma, which led to discontinuation of IP, was reported as SAE in a patient originally randomized in the benralizumab group in the DB period (the event was not considered serious and therefore no Investigator description was provided, however the event is included in SAE narratives). In addition, one (1.6%) patient in the placebo switched to benralizumab group was reported with SAE of malignancy (pancreatic carcinoma) during the OLE period (Pneumonia bacterial, Cellulitis, Urinary tract infection and Pulmonary artery thrombosis were also reported as SAEs for this patient). All events including pancreatic carcinoma were considered severe in intensity by the Investigator (except cellulitis), without relatedness to the investigational product.

## Helminth Infections

No patients reported helminth infections during the DB period.

During the OLE period, 1 patient (1.5%), adult in the benralizumab group reported 3 helminth infections (enterobiasis), of which 1 was serious, and all were possibly related to IP as assessed by the Investigator.

Hypersensitivity The overall proportion of patients with hypersensitivity AEs (Table 14.3.6.2) during the DB period was greater in the benralizumab group (10 [14.9%] patients) compared to the placebo group (4 [6.1%] patients). The most frequently reported hypersensitivity AEs during the DB period were rash (3 [4.5%] patients in the benralizumab group and no patients in the placebo group), rhinitis allergic (3 [4.5%] patients in the benralizumab group and 1 patient [1.5%] in the placebo group), and urticaria (2 [3.0%] patients in the benralizumab group and no patients in the in placebo group). No cases of anaphylaxis or anaphylactic reactions were reported during the DB period.

Benra=Benralizumab. DB=Double blind. IP=Investigational Product. IPD=Investigational Product Discontinuation. OLE=Open label extension. N=Number of patients in treatment group for the study period. PT=Preferred term. MedDRA version 28.0

On-study DB period is defined as AE started date on or after day of first DB IP dose up to the day prior first OLE IP inclusive for patients sa   r tt  't   tr   o  t  t r t    't   tr  ts who exit the study, or data cut-off date.

Number (%) of patients with a hypersensitivity event.

[a] Causally related to IP as assessment by the Investigator.

Hypersensitivity events are defined as preferred terms from the hypersensitivity narrow standard MedDRA query.

There was 1 hypersensitivity SAE (acute allergic reaction) reported during the DB period (1 day post dose 4) in a patient in the benralizumab group with a medical history of acne, allergic rhinitis, allergy (non-seasonal), anxiety, insomnia, iron deficiency anemia, migraine, and peptic ulcer. The patient's symptoms (itching, severe abdominal pain, nausea, and vomiting) resolved after using the epi-pen. The event was considered severe in intensity by the Investigator, though not related to the IP. Another patient in the benralizumab group reported a hypersensitivity AE 1 day after receiving treatment; the AE was assessed by the Investigator as possibly related to IP. There were no reports of hypersensitivity AEs which resulted in discontinuation of IP.

Twenty-five patients reported hypersensitivity events during the OLE period. For 4/25 patients, hypersensitivity had also been reported during the DB period, albeit for different AE. It is noteworthy that 13/25 patients with hypersensitivity reactions belonged to the placebo switched to benralizumab group in the OLE period.

For 2/25 patients, the hypersensitivity events were serious:

- one patient experienced an SAE of urticaria 7 day(s) after dose 16, which resulted in hospitalization; the SAE was considered moderate in intensity by the Investigator and not related to the IP.
- one patient with a history of allergies experienced an SAE of anaphylactic reaction following food consumption. The event which occurred 27 days after dose 32 was considered severe in intensity by the Investigator and not related to the IP.

## Injection Site Reactions

Injection site reactions were monitored following SC injection of IP treatment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## DB Period

During the DB period, injection site reaction AEs were reported in 3 (4.5%) patients in the benralizumab group and no patients in the placebo group (Table 30); all were transient in nature, and none resulted in discontinuation of IP. No serious injection site reaction AEs reported during the DB period. All injection site reaction AEs from the DB period were assessed by the Investigator as possibly related to IP and all were mild in intensity.

No consistent pattern or trend in the injection site reaction AEs was observed in the benralizumab group during the DB period based on injection site location or the number of IP administrations (Table 30).

## OLE Period

It should be noted that up to and including Visit 16, the IP was administered at the study site, on treatment visits and within visit windows. With the aim to reduce patient burden, IP could be administered after Visit 16 (week 54, OLE period) either on-site by an HCP or, optionally at home or a remote location by the patient or caregiver, in accordance with local health authorities' requirements/country.

There were no reports on AEs related to injection site reaction during the OLE period.

## Serious adverse event/deaths/other significant events

No new safety concerns regarding deaths, SAEs and other significant AEs were identified in this study compared with the known safety profile of benralizumab. It is noteworthy that the proportion of women with SAEs within the overall number of patients in NATRON was 67.7

## Deaths

One patient had an AE with an outcome of death during the DB period in the benralizumab group:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

On Day 52 after the first dose of IP, and 31 days from last dose before AE start date, a patient in the benralizumab group was hospitalised with a suspicion of enteritis infectious. The enteritis infection was not definitively diagnosed. Two days later the patient died from sepsis. The cause of sepsis is unknown. The patient was taking prednisolone (immunosuppressant drug) as a concomitant medication. The event was assessed as not possibly related to IP by the Investigator.

No AEs with an outcome of death were reported during the OLE period up to the DCO date 07 May 2025.

## Serious Adverse Events

Key SAEs with information on patients' treatment history in the study, the Investigator's assessment of the likelihood of a causal relationship between the SAE and the IP, and whether IPD or withdrawal from the study occurred have been provided in tabular format (Table 14.3.4.2). Additionally, the MAH has submitted full patient narratives.

The only SAE reported in the adolescent subpopulation during the DB period of the study was a patient experiencing an AE of HES flare 6 days after dose 3, likely secondary to viral upper respiratory tract infection, based on the Investigator's description. The event was assessed as not related to the IP.

## DB Period

There were no clinically meaningful differences in the frequency and event rate of SAEs (including events with the outcome = death) between the benralizumab and placebo groups during the DB period (Table 31). Most events were resolved and all events were assessed as not possibly related to IP by the Investigator.

Most events were assessed as not possibly related to IP by the Investigator and most events were resolved. There were 2 SAEs in the OLE period assessed as possibly related to the IP by the Investigator:

One SAE was an event of acquired haemophilia reported 18 days after dose 10 in a patient in the placebo switched to benralizumab group. The patient was hospitalized, and the study drug was discontinued. The patient was reported recovering, and has withdrawn from the study. The event was considered moderate in intensity and possibly related to study drug by the Investigator. The second SAE was an event of enterobiasis which has been discussed in previous section of the AR.

## Other Significant Adverse Events

No AEs were classified as other significant AEs in this study. Summaries of malignancy events, helminth infections, hypersensitivity events, and injection site reactions have been discussed in previous sections.

## Laboratory findings

All protocol-specified continuous laboratory parameters were summarized descriptively by absolute value at each visit by treatment group, together with the corresponding changes from baseline. Based on the clinical laboratory evaluations reported for the DB period, there were no clinically meaningful trends in laboratory parameters, and the proportions of patients with AEs related to laboratory parameters were low and similar between treatment groups.

No new safety concerns regarding clinical laboratory evaluations were identified.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Clinical laboratory results were presented separately for haematology, clinical chemistry, and urinalysis variables. Within each of these categories, results are examined in 3 ways: changes in mean values over time, changes in individual patients over time, and individual clinically important abnormalities.

## Haematology

## Changes in Haematology Values Over Time

There were no clinically meaningful trends between treatment groups over time in haematology variables assessed during the 24-week DB period, except for the recognized effects of benralizumab on depleting blood eosinophils and lowering blood basophil levels. Mean absolute basophil values remained within their reference range at all post-baseline timepoints.

## Changes in Haematology Values in Individual Subjects Over Time

Other than the expected changes in blood eosinophil count in benralizumab-treated patients, there were no clinically meaningful trends between treatment groups in haematology parameter shifts from baseline to maximum or minimum values post-baseline during the DB period.

## Individual Clinically Important Abnormalities in Haematology

The proportion of patients with haematology-related AEs under the blood and lymphatic system disorders SOC was low and generally similar between treatment groups during the DB period (Table 14.3.2.2.1).

The majority of haematology-related AEs during the DB period were mild or moderate and transient in nature and 1 AE of leukopenia in the benralizumab group was possibly related to IP by the Investigator.

One patient in each group had an AE of HES considered serious in the DB period. No patient had a haematology AE that led to discontinuation of IP.

## Clinical Chemistry

## Changes in Clinical Chemistry Values Over Time

There were no clinically meaningful trends between treatment groups in clinical chemistry values over time during the 24-week DB period.

## Changes in Clinical Chemistry Values in Individual Subjects Over Time

There were no clinically meaningful trends between treatment groups in clinical chemistry parameter shifts from baseline to maximum or minimum values post-baseline during the DB period.

Individual Clinically Important Abnormalities in Clinical Chemistry The proportion of patients with clinical chemistry-related AEs was low and similar between groups during the DB period. There was nothing clinically relevant to note for these AEs.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Urinalysis

Urinalysis assessments were initially performed in the study in accordance with the original CSP but were removed from the SoA in the amended CSP (CSP version 8) to reduce patient and site burden, as urinalysis results had been well characterised from multiple studies during product development. Based on the partial urinalysis data collected in the study for Glucose (mmol/L), Ketone Bodies (mmol/L), Occult Blood, Protein (g/L) &amp; Leukocytes (10 6 /L) no clinically meaningful trends in urinalysis parameters between treatment groups and no individual clinically important findings were identified in any group during the DB period.

## Vital Signs, Electrocardiograms, Physical Findings and Other Observations Related to Safety

Vital signs were reported for the DB period of the study.

## Vital Signs and ECG

There were no clinically meaningful trends in vital signs over time in either treatment group, and no notable differences in vital sign parameters between groups during the DB period. There were also no clinically meaningful shifts in vital sign parameters from baseline to post-baseline and no notable differences in vital sign shifts between groups during the DB period.

No notable differences were observed between groups during the DB period in ECG overall interpretation and no clinically meaningful shifts from baseline to last observation post-baseline. There was 1 (2.0%) patient in the benralizumab group with abnormal -clinically significant last observation post-baseline ECG value. In addition, there were no notable differences in QTcF or QTcB intervals between groups during the DB period.

Few patients reported AEs related to vital signs or ECGs, their incidences were similar between groups, and none of them were clinically significant. No new safety concerns regarding vital signs or ECG were identified.

## Physical Findings and Other Observations Related to Safety

Abnormal physical examination results were reported as AEs. One positive pregnancy test was reported in the placebo group in NATRON (Table 31). The outcome was fetal death (observed by ultrasound). No safety concern was identified based on the pregnancy and its outcome reported in the study.

## Safety in special populations

## Intrinsic factors

The effects of intrinsic factors on the adverse event profile of benralizumab were assessed by subgroup analyses of adverse events reported during the DB period in NATRON. The analysis by subgroups is based on the limited sample size from NATRON, supported further by the analysis of a larger safety dataset from asthma and EGPA studies and post-marketing experience No clinically meaningful trends were observed during the DB period in AEs in any category for the benralizumab group compared with the placebo group across the prespecified subgroups of age, sex, race, and geographic region as shown in Table 14.3.6.8 and summarized in Figure 14.3.6.10.
