The number of adolescents in NATRON is very limited. AEs occurred in all 4 patients in the subgroup &lt;18 years of age (3/3 patients in the benralizumab group and 1/1 in the placebo group). There were no SAEs or AEs leading to discontinuation of IP reported by adolescents during the study and no new ADRs were identified compared with the established safety profile of benralizumab.

## Use in Pregnancy and Lactation

One positive pregnancy test was reported in the placebo group in NATRON. The outcome was foetal death (observed by ultrasound). No safety concern was identified based on the pregnancy and its outcome reported in the study. The data on pregnancy exposure from completed and ongoing clinical studies are insufficient to inform on drug associated risk. Monoclonal antibodies, such as benralizumab, are transported across the placenta in a linear fashion as pregnancy progresses; therefore, a potential greater exposure of fetus is anticipated in the second and third trimester of pregnancy.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Additionally, there are no data on the excretion of benralizumab or its metabolites in human or animal milk and therefore, risk to the breastfed child cannot be excluded. The current SmPC already advises that, as a precautionary measure, it is preferable to avoid the use of Fasenra during pregnancy and that a decision must be made whether to discontinue breast-feeding or to discontinue/abstain from using Fasenra.

## Safety related to drug-drug interactions and other interactions

No new drug-drug interaction studies were submitted by the MAH. This was considered acceptable by the CHMP.

## Discontinuation due to adverse events

During the DB period, a fatal AE of sepsis occurred in 1 patient in the benralizumab group which was reported as discontinuation of study IP. No other AEs leading to discontinuation of IP were reported during the DB period.

In the OLE period, 1 patient in the benralizumab group had an AE of T-cell lymphoma leading to discontinuation of IP, and 1 patient in the placebo switched to benralizumab group with an AE of acquired haemophilia leading to discontinuation of IP. No other AEs leading to discontinuation of IP were reported in the OLE period.

No adolescents experienced an AE that led to discontinuation of IP during the study.

The event rates of AEs leading to discontinuation of IP were low during the DB and OLE periods.

## Post marketing experience

As of 13 May 2025, benralizumab is approved in more than 80 countries as an add-on treatment for severe eosinophilic asthma, and in more than 50 countries for EGPA. The cumulative global postmarketing patient exposure to benralizumab is estimated to be approximately 452,918 patient-years, from launch in 2017 to 30 April 2025. A summary of safety data from post-marketing sources is presented in periodic safety update reports (PSURs).

## 2.5.1. Discussion on clinical safety

The pivotal safety data of patients with HES submitted with the current application derives from the NATRON study where the safety and tolerability of benralizumab were investigated in patients with HES. The safety results from NATRON are supported by the safety and tolerability of benralizumab documented in the original submission of benralizumab for the treatment of severe eosinophilic asthma from long-term pivotal studies (SIROCCO, CALIMA and BORA) and EGPA, from Phase III study MANDARA where the Q4W dosing regimen was used.

Benralizumab was well-tolerated and any identified AEs were consistent with the known safety profile of benralizumab in adults. Nevertheless, the differences in eosinophil burden on asthma and HES patients and the clinical manifestations of HES ranging from benign to life-threatening disorders with the potential for significant end-organ damage are to be considered when discussing the need for safety data for the sought indication.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The MAH's committed to monitor long-term safety of adolescent patients with HES.

Safety and tolerability were evaluated in terms of AEs (frequency, relationship to IP, Intensity, discontinuation, seriousness, fatal events, vital signs and clinical laboratory assessments) as assessed by the Investigator. All safety analyses of the NATRON study for the DB period were performed using the Safety Analysis Set, which included all patients who received at least 1 dose of IP. All safety analyses for the OLE period were performed using the OLE Analysis Set, which included all patients who entered the OLE part of the study and who received at least 1 dose of benralizumab during this period.

Treatment with benralizumab in NATRON showed no new safety signals in patients with HES and was, in general, consistent with the known profile of benralizumab in severe eosinophilic asthma and EGPA.

The mean exposure during the double-blind period was similar between groups (for the 67 patients randomized to benralizumab group mean exposure was 140.4 days; for the 66 patients randomized to placebo group 138.1 days). One hundred and twenty-six patients entered the OLE period; the median OLE duration for the benralizumab group was 595 days and 586 days for the placebo switched to benralizumab group.

Overall similar incidences of AEs and SAEs were reported between groups; the proportion of patients with AEs assessed as possibly related to IP was greater with benralizumab (11 patients, 16.4%) compared to placebo 5 (7.6%) patient. The most frequently reported AE with a numerical imbalance was headache (11[16.4%] patients in the benralizumab group vs 5 [7.6%] in the placebo group.) Headache is a known TEAE for benralizumab albeit with a lower reported frequency in the phase III exacerbation studies (40 [1.6%] patients) and is included in the current prescribing information. The proportion of patients with severe AEs was 6 (9.0%) in the benralizumab group versus 5 (7.6%) in the placebo group. A fatal AE of sepsis during the DB period occurred in a patient in the benralizumab group and was assessed as not related to IP. The fatal event was reported as a discontinuation of study IP.

Hypersensitivity events were reported more frequently with benralizumab in the DB period (10 [14.9%] patients), compared with placebo group (4 patients [6.1%]); across the DB+OLE periods combined, 25 patients reported hypersensitivity during the OLE of which 13/25 belonged to the placebo-switched group. Discussion on the incidence of hypersensitivity in patients with HES treated with benralizumab with respect to information from the MAH's safety database was requested. The MAH presented the available safety data on Hypesensitivity AEs tabled as % number of patients and event rates for both treatment groups reported in the DB, OLE and DB+OLE periods of the NATRON study and additional safety data from previous studies for asthma (Sirocco &amp; Calima) and EGPA. Overall, in NATRON, hypersensitivity was more frequently reported with benralizumab than placebo during the DB period, but event rates were lower in the OLE than in the DB period. When looking at the total time on benralizumab during the DB + OLE period, the exposure-adjusted event rates were generally similar to the exposure-adjusted event rates for placebo during the DB period as well as that seen during previous studies for asthma and EGPA.

Injection site reactions in the DB period occurred in 3 patients (4.5%) on benralizumab and 0 on placebo; no injection-site AEs were reported in the OLE. Hypersensitivity and injection site reactions are already adequately included in the current prescribing information.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Malignancy reports included one SAE of chronic eosinophilic leukaemia in the benralizumab DB group 1(1.5%) patient, two non-serious malignancy AEs in benralizumab patients during the OLE period (2 [3.1%] patients), and one SAE of pancreatic carcinoma in the placebo-switched group during the OLE (1 1.6%] patient). In addition, 1(1.5%) patient in the placebo switched to benralizumab OLE group reported three helminth infections (enterobiasis), one being serious and all assessed as possibly related to IP. Two OLE SAEs were considered possibly related to IP by the Investigator (acquired haemophilia and enterobiasis) and led to discontinuation.

Laboratory, clinical chemistry, ECG, and vital signs showed no clinically meaningful trends between groups. The known pharmacodynamic effect of eosinophil depletion was observed overall; the MAH was asked to discuss the mean absolute eosinophil count at Week 16 (470 cells/µL) with respect to seroconversion. The MAH clarified that the elevated AEC mean value of blood eosinophil counts at the Week 16 time point is primarily driven by a single patient with a diagnosis of chronic eosinophilic leukaemia and a markedly elevated value of eosinophil counts (26,950 cells/μL) at Week 16.

Analysis of ADA responses demonstrated no clinically meaningful difference in AE or SAE rates: 5/7 (71.4%) patients reported an AE during DB period among treatment-emergent ADA-positive benralizumab patients, versus 38/59 (64.4%) in ADA-negative patients; hypersensitivity was reported in 1/7 (14.3%) ADA-positive versus 9/59 (15.3%) ADA-negative patients. No anaphylaxis linked to ADA positivity was observed.

Discontinuations due to AEs were rare and included the DB fatal sepsis case as well as two discontinuations in the OLE period (T-cell lymphoma and acquired haemophilia). The event rate of SAEs for the benralizumab group and the placebo switched to benralizumab group was lower during the OLE period (8.95 and 8.96, respectively) than for the benralizumab group during the DB period (16.35).

## Assessment of paediatric data on clinical safety

A total of 4/134 (3.0%) patients were adolescents aged ≥ 12 to &lt; 18 years of age (3 patients received benralizumab and 1 patient received placebo) in the NATRON study. Treatment with benralizumab in patients with HES, including adolescents, was overall well tolerated with no new safety findings. AEs occurred in all 4 patients in the subgroup &lt;18 years of age (3/3 patients in the benralizumab group and 1/1 in the placebo group). There were no SAEs or AEs leading to discontinuation of IP reported by adolescents during the study and no new ADRs were identified compared with the established safety profile of benralizumab. The only SAE reported in the adolescent subpopulation during the DB period of the study was a patient experiencing an AE of HES flare 6 days after dose 3, likely secondary to viral upper respiratory tract infection, based on the Investigator's description. The event was assessed as not related to the IP.

Based on the available safety data, it should be highlighted that effect of benralizumab on the subgroup of patients in the age GROUP 12-18 years was assessed on a very limited number of adolescents compared to adults with HES in the DB period of NATRON. Adolescents are targeted to complete a minimum of 2 years of treatment in the ongoing OLE period, however no information had been provided on the duration of exposure of this patient subgroup, in the OLE period.

Accordingly, the MAH provided updated safety analysis from available AE reports in the paediatric population (&lt; 18 years of age) from clinical trials (including also data from patients 6-12 years old) and post marketing experience. Benralizumab was well-tolerated and any identified AEs were consistent with the known safety profile of benralizumab in adults.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Additionally, the MAH provided post-marketing safety data split by age and/or body weight to support the safety of benralizumab in adolescents with HES, however, body weight was reported as unknown for 360/421 AE case reports in adolescents and 53/72 in children (&lt;12 years). The data were primarily obtained from US where benralizumab is approved only for severe eosinophilic asthma from the age of 6 years. The most frequently reported AEs in patients &lt;18 years included: Respiratory AEs (asthma, Dyspnoea, Wheezing), General AEs (Headache, Cough, Nause, Pyrexia) and non-medical events (Offlabel use, Drug ineffectiveness and Product dose omission issues). A safety analysis was performed on cases with body weight stratified into &lt;35 kg and ≥35 kg subgroups: for patients &lt;18 years of age and weight &lt;35 kg, in 12 post-marketing reports aseptic meningitis, asthma, pneumonia, seizures and pneumothorax were reported as serious adverse events.

Taking the low number of adolescent patients in the NATRON study into account as well as the limited information on exposure of patients in this age group the MAH was asked to provide an extrapolation exercise in accordance with ICH E11A (please refer to discussion on clinical efficacy further above).

Based on the PopPK modelling which covered a range of 30 to 110 kg, the adolescents at the lower body weight (30-40 kg) exhibited higher exposures and related safety information is considered limited. It is also noted that the additional post marketing safety data provided by the MAH mainly included children from the US (with asthma), where the posology in children 6-11 years of age is different depending on weight (10 mg Q8W if below 35 kg and 30 mg Q8W if above 35 kg, as maintenance treatment), which is lower than the 30 mg Q4W proposed for HES treatment. In addition, weight was only reported for a minority of the patients.

Taking into consideration this observation and the limited safety information in subjects &lt;18 years and weight &lt;35 kg, a statement addressing the uncertainty on the safety profile of adolescents with &lt;35 kg body weight was added in SmPC 4.2 (see below) and the MAH agreed to amend the indication to include only patients with at least 35 kg body weight.

In view of the small safety database the collection of additional data planned by the MAH to address the main uncertainty, namely the very limited number of adolescents exposed to benralizumab in NATRON (n=4) is endorsed and the submission is recommended as soon as they become available: long-term follow-up through NATRON open-label extension (OLE) with analysis data from 12-52 months of exposure, long-term safety data from the open-label ROSY-F rollover study (for participants who have completed a previous study with benralizumab) and a paediatric basket study (CLIPS) aiming to enrol ≥14 paediatric HES and EGPA patients aged 6-&lt;18 years to inform the PK, PD, safety and efficacy of benralizumab in adolescents with HES and EGPA.

## 2.5.2. Conclusions on clinical safety

Treatment with benralizumab in NATRON demonstrated a safety profile consistent with prior experience in severe eosinophilic asthma and EGPA. No new adverse drug reactions were identified. Overall rates of adverse events and serious adverse events during the double-blind period were similar between benralizumab and placebo, most reported events were moderate and transient, and the majority were assessed as not possibly related to investigational product.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Concerns on the adequacy of data provided from a limited number of adolescents with HES were addressed by post-marketing safety data split by age and/or body weight primarily obtained from US where benralizumab is approved only for severe eosinophilic asthma from the age of 6 years. Taking into consideration the Pop-PK data where adolescents exhibited higher exposures at the body weight 30-40 kg and the limited safety information in subjects &lt;18 years and weight &lt;35 kg, the safety of administering 30 mg SC Q4W benralizumab to patients &gt; 12 years with HES is considered demonstrated with the exception of adolescents with a body weight below 35 kg. The indication was amended accordingly.

## 2.5.3. PSUR cycle

The requirements for submission of periodic safety update reports for this medicinal product are set out in the list of Union reference dates (EURD list) provided for under Article 107c(7) of Directive 2001/83/EC and any subsequent updates published on the European medicines web-portal.

The MAH submitted an updated RMP version 8.1 with this application.

The PRAC considered that the risk management plan version 8.1 is acceptable.

## 2.7. Update of the Product information

As a consequence of this new indication, sections 4.1, 4.2, 4.4, 4.8, 5.1 and 5.2 of the SmPC have been updated. Particularly, a new warning with regard to Fasenra has not been studied in patients with active life-threatening manifestations of HES (see section 4.2) has been added to the product information. For detailed changes to the SmPC please refer to the full PI in attachment. The Package Leaflet has been updated accordingly.

Changes were also made to the PI to bring it in line with the current Agency/QRD template, SmPC guideline and other relevant guideline(s) [e.g. Excipients guideline, storage conditions, Braille, etc…], which were reviewed and accepted by the CHMP.

Section 6.5 Nature and contents of container of the SPC was updated to include editorial changes in the description of both pre-filled syringe and pre-filled pen. Both editorial updates were considered in compliance with the information included in section R Regional Information for Benralizumab Autoinjector (30 mg) and accessorized prefilled syringe (APFS) and therefore agreed.

The added advice to 4.2 of the SmPC to spare 5 cm area around the navel for injections is agreed as it constitutes standard practise.

In addition, the list of local representatives in the PL has been revised to delete contact details for the representative(s) of the United Kingdom (Northern Ireland).

## 2.7.1. User consultation

A justification for not performing a full user consultation with target patient groups on the package leaflet has been submitted by the MAH and has been found acceptable for the following reasons:

The proposed changes are not substantial enough to warrant further user consultation and the readability report provided during the initial marketing authorization application is relevant for this application.

## 3. Benefit-Risk Balance

## 3.1. Therapeutic Context

## 3.1.1. Disease or condition

HES is a heterogeneous disorder, with varied organ system involvement and diverse symptoms that range in severity. The most common presenting signs and symptoms reflect cutaneous, pulmonary, and gastrointestinal involvement, though any organ can be involved. Eosinophilia is defined by increased eosinophils in the peripheral blood and is observed in several inflammatory and reactive conditions. Blood eosinophilia is defined by an AEC of more than 0.5 × 109/L, whereas hypereosinophilia (HE) requires an AEC of ≥1.5 × 109/L. In patients with persistent (or recurrent) HE, tissue infiltration by eosinophils and release of eosinophil-derived mediators and cytotoxic proteins may result in clinically relevant organ damage and thus a hypereosinophilic syndrome (HES). In accordance with the definition provided by the International Cooperative Working Group on Eosinophil Disorders (ICOG-EO) in 2022 (Valent et al., 2023), HES is defined by (i) the presence of blood and/or tissue HE, (ii) HE-associated organ damage, and (iii) exclusion of another underlying disorder or pathology as the primary driver of organ damage.

HES is diagnosed when known causes of secondary hypereosinophilia are excluded. HES can be classified into several subtypes, including myeloproliferative, lymphocytic and idiopathic variants (MHES, L-HES and I-HES, respectively), based on distinct molecular characteristics: Myeloid HES (M-HES) is predominantly associated with the FIP1-like 1 (FIP1L1) platelet-derived growth factor receptor alpha (PDGFRA) fusion gene; in lymphocytic HES (L-HES), hypereosinophilia is driven by a clonal population of activated T cells that overproduce eosinophilopoietic cytokines, most notably interleukin (IL)-5; in idiopathic HES (I-HES) the mechanisms resulting in eosinophil expansion remain unknown.

## 3.1.2. Available therapies and unmet medical need

Current management of patients who have active symptomatic HES is based on long-term systemic OCS (Ogbogu et al 2009), the exception being for the myeloproliferative variant of HES associated with the FIP1L1-PDGFRA fusion tyrosine kinase gene translocation, for which imatinib mesylate is approved and considered first-line therapy (Dispenza and Bochner 2018, Curtis and Ogbogu 2016, Khoury et al 2016). Although most patients (85% to 90%) with the non-myeloproliferative variant have an initial response to corticosteroids, their use is subsequently discontinued in approximately 40% of patients due to lack of efficacy and in an additional 20% of patients due to intolerance (Khoury et al 2018, Ogbogu et al 2009). Chronic use of corticosteroids leads to considerable morbidity, and attempts are made to reduce doses due to steroid toxicity and related AEs, (Buchman 2001). Steroid-sparing immunosuppressive agents such as hydroxyurea, IFN-α, and methotrexate (Dispenza and Bochner 2018) are often introduced as second-line agents, but these are also associated with poor tolerability.

Currently, mepolizumab 300 mg Q4W is the only biologic approved for adults with inadequately controlled HES without an identifiable non-haematologic secondary cause. Mepolizumab is an IL-5 antagonist, which binds to IL-5 thereby preventing it from binding to the IL-5Rα complex on the eosinophil cell surface. In a Phase III registrational trial, mepolizumab reduced the proportion of patients experiencing 1 or more flares during the study compared with placebo, together with improvements in the time to first flare, annualised flare rate, and fatigue severity. As not all patients experienced benefit with mepolizumab, there remains a significant unmet need in this patient population.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.1.3. Main clinical studies

## NATRON study

A Multicentre, Randomised, Double-blind, Parallel-group, Placebo-controlled, 24 Week Phase III Study with an Open-label Extension to Evaluate the Efficacy and Safety of Benralizumab in Patients With Hypereosinophilic Syndrome (HES).

|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| D3254C00001 (NATRON) AstraZeneca EudraCT No: 2019-002039-27 NCT No: NCT04191304 | A multicentre, randomised, double-blind, parallel-group, placebo controlled, 24 week Phase III study with an open-label extension to evaluate the efficacy and safety of benralizumab in patients with hypereosinophilic syndrome (HES) | To evaluate the effect of benralizumab on the time to first HES worsening/flare in the DB period | Time to first HES worsening/ flare in the DB period | Benralizumab 30 mg SC Q4W (N=67) Placebo SC Q4W (N=67)                          | Female or male patients aged 12 years and above who have documented HES, negative for the FIP1L1- PDGFRA fusion tyrosine kinase gene translocation, have stable HES treatment dose(s) and regimen for ≥ 4 weeks at the time of Visit 1, have AEC ≥ 1000 cells/μL at Visit 1, and have signs or symptoms of HES worsening/flare and/or laboratory abnormalities indicative of HES worsening/flare at Visit 1 or a documented history of 2 or more HES worsenings/flares within 12 months prior to Visit 1. | A 24-week DB, placebo- controlled treatment period An ongoing OLE period, involving treatment with benralizumab for at least 1 year in adults and 2 years in adolescents |

## 3.2. Favourable effects

Benralizumab demonstrated consistent efficacy over placebo in patients with HES during the 24-week double blind period: significant reduction was demonstrated in the risk of first HES worsening/flare by 65% (HR 0.35), the proportion of patients experiencing a flare (22.4% vs 45.5%), and the annualized flare rate by 66% (rate ratio 0.34). In addition, in patients treated with benralizumab a delayed hematologic relapse with a 92% reduction in risk (HR 0.08) was reported as well as a statistically significant improvement in fatigue (PROMIS Fatigue Short Form 7a) at Week 24. In patients with HES, depletion of blood eosinophils was consistent with the effect observed in asthma and EGPA trials. Blood eosinophil depletion was seen at the first observed timepoint (Week 4) and was maintained over the 24-week treatment period. Moreover, improvement was reported on the control of background therapy and several quality of life and healthcare utilization measures; treatment effects were consistent across predefined subgroups though subgroup estimates carry greater uncertainty due to small numbers.

## 3.3. Uncertainties and limitations about favourable effects

The MAH clarified during the procedure that the number of available biopsies in NATRON was insufficient to support firm conclusions or inform whether blood hypereosinophilia was accompanied by tissue hypereosinophilia. The MAH indicated that radiographic evidence for identification of organ involvement was not available. However, it was confirmed that all randomised patients were responsive to anti-inflammatory therapy which highlights corticosteroid responsiveness as a key characteristic of the target population.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

With respect to the age group of interest (12-17 years old patients with HES), 1/3 adolescents who received benralizumab experienced a HES worsening/flare. The only adolescent in the placebo group also experienced a HES worsening/flare. Due to low recruitment of patients below 12-18 years of age in NATRON (only three adolescents were randomized to the benralizumab group and one to the placebo group), the MAH reported the efficacy results from an extended subgroup of patients aged ≤ 21 years (n=16). Two out of nine (22.2%) patients in the benralizumab group and 3/7 (42.9%) patients in the placebo group &lt; 21 years of age experienced a HES worsening/flare during the DB period consistent with the results from the overall study population. Still, the proportion of patients ≤21 with HES flare/worsening by week 12 was higher in the benralizumab group compared to the placebo group in patients ≤21 years of age.

To give further support on the adolescent population the MAH performed an extrapolation assessment with the objective to evaluate whether efficacy and safety data for benralizumab in adults can be applied to adolescents with HES, in accordance with ICH E11A. The MAH discussed available evidence to support the similarity between adult and paediatric HES on pathophysiology, diagnostic criteria, clinical presentation and disease phenotype. The MAH also provided information from RWE data on the similarity of the overall pattern of organ involvement between adults and adolescents with HES, although with pronounced differences in frequency. The limited approved therapeutic options for paediatric patients with HES (systemic corticosteroids as first-line therapy) with recognised long-term toxicity were discussed. Additionally, the MAH submitted the results from the analysis of real-world data in adults and adolescents treated with mepolizumab (IL-5-targeted therapy) showing comparable haematological remission rates.

Efficacy of adolescents with HES weighing at least 35 kg was demonstrated by extrapolation from adult population with HES, in accordance with ICH E11A.

## 3.4. Unfavourable effects

Treatment with benralizumab in NATRON showed no new safety signals in patients with HES and was, in general, consistent with the known profile of benralizumab in severe eosinophilic asthma and EGPA.

Post-marketing safety data split by age and/or body weight were provided to support the safety of benralizumab in adolescents with HES obtained mainly from US where benralizumab is approved only for severe eosinophilic asthma from the age of 6 years. Body weight was reported as unknown for 360/421 AE case reports in adolescents and 53/72 in children (&lt;12 years). The most frequently reported AEs in patients &lt;18 years included: Respiratory AEs (asthma, Dyspnoea, Wheezing), General AEs (Headache, Cough, Nause, Pyrexia) and non-medical events (Off-label use, Drug ineffectiveness and Product dose omission issues). A safety analysis was performed on cases with body weight stratified into &lt;35 kg and ≥35 kg subgroups: for patients &lt;18 years of age and weight &lt;35 kg, in 12 post-marketing reports aseptic meningitis, asthma, pneumonia, seizures and pneumothorax were reported as serious adverse events.

Additionally, the MAH provided PopPK modeling data in the context of ICH E11A to support the administration of 30 mg Q4W benralizumab as a safe and effective dose for adolescents with HES. The PopPK modeling data which covered a body-weight range of 30 to 110 kg demonstrated that the adolescents at the lower body weight (30-40 kg) exhibited higher exposures. Taking into consideration this observation and the limited safety information in subjects &lt;18 years and weight &lt;35 kg, this uncertainty on the safety profile of adolescents was addressed by restricting the indication to patients of at least 35 kg body weight. With the exception of patients with a body weight&lt;35 kg, the safety of benralizumab has been demonstrated in adults and adolescents with HES.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Treatment-emergent ADA response developed in 7 out of 66 (11%) patients treated with benralizumab during the phase 3 placebo-controlled 24-week treatment period. A total of 2 (3%) patients in the benralizumab group developed neutralising antibodies.

## 3.5. Uncertainties and limitations about unfavourable effects

The Safety Analysis Set in NATRON included 67 patients who received benralizumab and 66 patients who received placebo during the DB period. Sixty-five patients were treated with benralizumab 30 mg Q4W SX for at least 24 weeks. Up to the primary analysis DCO, 126 patients received at least one dose of IP in the OLE period and were included in the OLE Analysis Set. The mean exposure during the double blind period was similar between groups (for the 67 patients randomized to benralizumab group mean exposure was 140.4 days; for the 66 patients randomized to placebo group 138.1 days). The median OLE duration for the benralizumab group was 595 days and 586 days for the placebo switched to benralizumab group. NATRON is ongoing; Taking into consideration the small size of the study, additional safety data are collected during treatment follow-up in the OLE period, including analysis from 12-52 months of exposure, long-term safety data from the open-label ROSY-F rollover study (for participants who have completed a previous study with benralizumab) and a paediatric basket study (CLIPS) aiming to enrol ≥14 paediatric HES and EGPA patients aged 6-&lt;18 years to inform the PK, PD, safety and efficacy of benralizumab in adolescents with HES and EGPA will help the further characterisation of the safety profile of benralizumab in patients with HES and the MA holder is recommended to submit these data post-authorisation.

Patients with severe and life-threatening HES and/or HES complications were excluded from NATRON and therefore a warning statements to evaluate patients who develop live-threatening manifestations of HES for the need for continued therapy with benralizumab was added in the prescribing information.

Safety data relevant to adolescents (12-18 years of age) were very limited (only 4 adolescents enrolled in NATRON: 3 benralizumab, 1 placebo). Whilst the data, in conjunction with the provided extrapolation exercise are considered acceptable for marketing authorization the indication was restricted to patient over 35kg.

## 3.7. Benefit-risk assessment and discussion

## 3.7.1. Importance of favourable and unfavourable effects

HES is a rare, debilitating disorder characterised by varied organ system involvement and diverse symptoms that range in severity. Dermatologic involvement is the most common clinical manifestation followed by pulmonary, gastrointestinal, neurologic and cardiac manifestations. Most patients also have constitutional symptoms (eg, fatigue, fever, malaise, weight loss, night sweats), which can be debilitating. HES can have severe sequela depending on the organ involved.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Benralizumab demonstrated in the NATRON study consistent efficacy over placebo in patients with HES during the 24-week double-blind period, with a statistically significant reduction in the risk of first HES worsening/flare, the proportion of patients experiencing a flare and the annualised flare rate.

Treatment with benralizumab in NATRON demonstrated a safety profile consistent with prior experience in severe eosinophilic asthma and EGPA. New adverse drug reactions have not been identified. Overall rates of adverse events and serious adverse events during the double-blind period were similar between benralizumab and placebo and the majority of reported AEs were moderate and transient.

## 3.7.2. Balance of benefits and risks

The efficacy and safety data from the NATRON study demonstrated a clear benefit as an add-on treatment for adult and adolescent patients aged 12 years and older weighing at least 35 kg with inadequately controlled hypereosinophilic syndrome without an identifiable non-haematologic secondary cause (see section 4.2 and 5.1). This is reflected in a significant reduction in the risk of first HES worsening/flare by 65% (HR 0.35), the proportion of patients experiencing a flare (22.4% vs 45.5%), and the annualized flare rate by 66% (rate ratio 0.34). In addition, in patients treated with benralizumab a delayed hematologic relapse with a 92% reduction in risk (HR 0.08) was reported as well as a statistically significant improvement in fatigue (PROMIS Fatigue Short Form 7a) at Week 24. The safety data displays an acceptable risk profile being in general consistent with the known safety profile of benralizumab.

## 3.7.3. Additional considerations on the benefit-risk balance

## 3.8. Conclusions

The overall B/R of Fasenra as an add-on treatment for adult and adolescent patients aged 12 years and older weighing at least 35 kg with inadequately controlled hypereosinophilic syndrome without an identifiable non-haematologic secondary cause (see section 4.2 and 5.1) is positive.

## 4. Recommendations

## Outcome

Based on the review of the submitted data, the CHMP considers the following variation acceptable and therefore recommends the variation to the terms of the Marketing Authorisation, concerning the following changes:

| Variation(s) requested   | Variation(s) requested                                                              | Type              |
|--------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------|
| C.I.6.a                  | C.I.6.a Addition of a new therapeutic indication or modification of an approved one | Variation type II |

Extension of indication to include treatment of adults and adolescents with hypereosinophilic syndrome (HES) for FASENRA, based on primary analysis from study D3254C00001 (NATRON); this is a multicentre, randomised, double-blind, parallel-group, placebo-controlled, 24-week phase III study with an open-label extension to evaluate the efficacy and safety of benralizumab in patients with HES;

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

As a consequence, sections 4.1, 4.2, 4.4, 4.8, 5.1 and 5.2 of the SmPC are updated. The Package Leaflet is updated in accordance. Version 8.1 of the RMP has been approved. In addition, the Marketing authorisation holder (MAH) took the opportunity to introduce editorial and administrative updates to the PI and to update the list of local representatives in the Package Leaflet. Furthermore, editorial changes were included into section 6.5 of the SmPC.

The variation leads to amendments to the annexes I, IIIB and to the Risk Management Plan (RMP).

## Amendments to the marketing authorisation

In view of the data submitted with the variation, amendments to Annex I, IIIB and to the Risk Management Plan are recommended.

Furthermore, the CHMP reviewed the available paediatric data of studies subject to the agreed Paediatric Investigation Plan EMA/PE/0000253868 and the results of these studies are reflected in the Summary of Product Characteristics (SmPC) and, as appropriate, the Package Leaflet.

## Reminders to the MAH

1. In accordance with Article 13(3) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004 the Agency makes available a European Public Assessment Report (EPAR) on the medicinal product assessed by the Committee for Medicinal Products for Human Use. The EPAR is first published after the granting of the initial marketing authorisation (MA) and is continuously updated during the lifecycle of the medicinal product. In particular, following a major change to the MA, the Agency further publishes the assessment report of the CHMP and the reasons for its opinion in favour of granting the change to the authorisation, after deletion of any information of a commercially confidential nature.

Should you consider that the CHMP assessment report contains commercially confidential information, please provide the EMA Procedure Assistant your proposal for deletion of commercially confidential information (CCI) in \"track changes\" and with detailed justification by 5 June 2026. The principles to be applied for the deletion of CCI are published on the EMA website at https://www.ema.europa.eu/en/documents/other/heads-medicines-agencies/europeanmedicines-agency-guidance-document-identification-commercially-confidential-information\\_en.pdf

In addition, should you consider that the CHMP assessment report contains personal data, please provide the EMA Procedure Assistant your proposal for deletion of these data in \"track changes\" and with detailed justification by 5 June 2026. We would like to remind you that, according to Article 4(1) of Regulation (EU) 2016/679 (General Data Protection Regulation, \"GDPR\") 'personal data' means any information, relating to an identified or identifiable natural person (the 'data subject'). An identifiable natural person is one who can be identified, directly or indirectly, in particular by reference to an identifier such as a name, an identification number, location data, an online identifier or to one or more factors specific to the physical, physiological, genetic, mental, economic, cultural or social identity of that natural person.

It is important to clarify that pseudonymised data are also considered personal data. According to Article 4(5) of GDPR pseudonymisation means that personal data is processed in a manner that the personal data can no longer be attributed to a specific data subject without the use of additional information (e.g. key-coded data).

Accordingly, the name and the patient identification number are two examples of personal data which may relate to an identified or identifiable natural person. The definitions also encompass for instance: office e-mail address or phone number of a company, data concerning health, e.g. information in medical records, clinical reports or case narratives which relates to an identifiable individual.\"

2. The MAH is reminded to submit an eCTD closing sequence with the final documents provided by Eudralink during the procedure (including final PI translations, if applicable) within 15 days after the Commission Decision, if there will be one within 2 months from adoption of the CHMP Opinion, or prior to the next regulatory activity, whichever is first. If the Commission Decision will be adopted within 12 months from CHMP Opinion, the closing sequence should be submitted within 30 days after the Opinion. For additional guidance see chapter 4.1 of the Harmonised Technical Guidance for eCTD Submissions in the EU.
